@@138599523 <p> Over 40 years painter Nigel Brown 's works have commentated on New Zealand 's past , present and future . He tells Rebecca Fox about the journey . <p> It is a story that begins in the 1980s in a practically abandoned Auckland council-owned building . <p> A young Nigel Brown was living in his painting studio . <p> " I was a bit down on my luck , ' ' Brown says as he settles into his chair in his studio in Dunedin 's harbour-side industrial district . <p> Winter sun floods in the large floor-to-ceiling first floor windows , artworks are stacked around the walls , an inspiration wall is filled with newspaper clippings and sketches , and tables are piled high with books and discarded paint tubes . <p> He reminisces about his old Auckland studio , a messy , dusty , broken down place he ended up living in after becoming a full-time painter in 1978 on the back of the success of his sell-out show of Lemon Tree paintings . It is from this period his latest survey show , " Nigel Brown Broadly @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on a grim note , I suppose , with a man 's head on a plate with a woman holding it " . <p> The work Judith and Holfoferns ( 1982 ) is a take on the Old Testament heroine that appealed to feminists of the 1980s . Another of his early works included is Clairmont Painting ( 1984 ) featuring his friend , neo-expressionist artist Philip Clairmont ( 1949-84 ) . <p> But what he really dug into at that time was the anti-nuclear movement . Brown was one of the founding members of Visual Artists Against the Nuclear Age . His works culminated in his one and only exhibition at the Dunedin Public Art Gallery , the 1985 exhibition " Living in the Bomb Age " . <p> " It was a bit controversial , it had male nudity in it . They were big paintings . They were exploring all sorts of ideas to do with the nuclear thing ; it was a pretty passionate movement in New Zealand at the time . ' ' <p> Brown , who graduated from Elam School of Art at the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ major issues of the times using his work to commentate on topics as wide as the 1981 Springbok tour , climate change , conservation , human rights , peace and poverty . <p> " I 'm certainly looking for new ways to speak , sometimes about past things and bring them up again . So you might find in here a painting that appears to be about the Pink and White Terraces but it 's quite a complicated painting when you read it . ' ' <p> Author and poet Fiona Farrell , someone Brown has long admired , writes about his work as " not hanging quietly " , in an essay on his latest exhibition . <p> " They argue and interrupt , they tease and puzzle , they open onto the landscape of these islands with passionate commitment . ' ' <p> PHOTOS : CRAIG BAXTER <p> Brown 's style of painting and use of words came in for intense criticism in his early years as a painter . <p> Brown began to use words and phrases in his paintings as his years working in factories and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the street and engaging with " the words at least ' ' . <p> " So , I 've used the vernacular at different times . Because there 's a lot of people that are culturally deprived out there . And one thing that drives me on is that unique objects are what I 've got to give to people . So I 'm trying to give people the comfort of the hand done . " <p> In an essay on Brown 's work for the exhibition , Dr David Craig says Brown has marshalled a large range of " iconic and symbolic ' ' elements in his paintings since the 1970s . <p> " Rendered via a rough , direct handling of paint , in a flattened perspective , almost woodblock-cartoon manner , with multilayered framings , often including prophetic declarations or critical questions in texts . <p> " Thus Brown has staged and dramatised significant elements and issues in his own literally iconic framings . ' ' <p> Brown 's style of painting and his use of words came in for some criticism in his early years as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ poet and his history with the South Island , in particular Lowburn Station where he was brought up , influenced his use of text . <p> His father and grandfather were born on Kyeburn Diggings and mined for gold . His father trained as a bomber pilot and returned to Central Otago after the war with Brown 's mother , who was from Whanganui . They tried to settle in the South but the climate did not suit his mother 's disabilities so they moved north to finally settle on an orchard in Tauranga . <p> " My father wrote about all of this . He was quite critical of the closed-off world he 'd come from . And he was quite depressed when the Clyde Dam happened . ' ' <p> Optimism . ( 2014 ) <p> His father 's poetry has inspired him to work with poets throughout his career , often illustrating their works , most recently Glenn Colquhoun 's Ballad of Joe Taihape . He has also worked with James K Baxter , Bill Manhire , Brian Gregory , David Eggelton and has had others write @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> His use of Baxter in his paintings has been questioned in more recent times after a letter by Baxter admitting raping his wife was published . <p> " It absolutely turns you off . But you have to look a little bit longer at what I do because I always use Baxter as a symbol . I 've used him often as a symbol of the poet , just a question in a person . ' ' <p> His early life and work also led to the creation of one of Brown 's many trademarks , a male figure in a black singlet , a motif that started in the Lemon Tree paintings . <p> " The reason I related to the black singlet as a person was that I was brought up on orchards , so there was a lot of manual labour going on and in the 1970s I worked in factories , a sawmill out in Riverhead Auckland . ' ' <p> Climate change is another strong focus of his work , something which began with being awarded the inaugural Artists to Antarctica Award in 1998 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the term used by glaciologists . <p> Those ideas came to the forefront again when Brown and his partner Sue moved to Cosy Nook in Southland after discovering land was much cheaper than their intended destination in Northland . <p> " We like to go down back roads and find places . ' ' <p> A conversation between H Tuwhare and Amy Bock . <p> It was in Southland he witnessed the sea erode the land . " Erosion is natural on a certain level , but when it becomes out of control , you feel threatened , or most people do feel threatened . ' ' <p> For a few years , the couple commuted between Auckland and Southland before finally committing to Southland and buying the land . <p> " The only thing that had cushioned me for the bottom of the South Island was that I 'd been to Antarctica . I remember standing on Gemstone Beach and going , you ai n't going to be able to live here . ' ' <p> Shifting south was " quite a performance for an artist with lots of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ essentially having their own beach . <p> " You might think it was completely remote down there but lots of people did come and visit . We were a bit of an oddity to the locals . ' ' <p> It also gave him the opportunity to build a large studio and the couple indulged in their love of second-hand shops , even creating a Crown Lynn room in their home . They also had a small herd of Highland cattle to keep the grass down on the property . <p> Brown completed the Hump Ridge Track for his 60th birthday , intrigued that locals had built the thousands of steps to create the walk . <p> During his time in the South , he took advantage of artist trips to Stewart Island and Dusky Sound which continued to feed his use of Captain Cook imagery . <p> " The Cook thing has been a particularly rich thing for me because you 're actually working through , as time progresses , New Zealanders ' attitudes to history which has changed a bit . People used to absent-mindedly say Cook was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ would n't say now . There is a mushrooming of awareness that we are an extension of Pacifica . ' ' <p> Brown , who was named an Officer of the New Zealand Order of Merit for services to painting and print making in 2004 , has been wondering recently why he has been fixated on Cook . <p> " It 's to do with the two ideas of life that some of us like , order and uniform . And society encourages these perfect myths about people . And my father was in a uniform . And the first thing when he got off the plane after having been overseas , done all his training and he got into Central Otago , his father looked at him and said get those bloody blinkers off . Because he 'd been wearing sunglasses . ' ' <p> For the past 10 years the couple have lived in Dunedin with Brown using it as a base for his artist trips away . Dunedin does not feature often in his works as he believes plenty of other artists have that territory covered including @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Hotere , to name a few . <p> " I 'll do a side trip to somewhere like Haast again and get ideas . I 'm at that stage in my life where I 'm going over material and reworking it in a different way . ' ' <p> Listening to ruru. ( 2023 ) <p> Mixing aspects of New Zealand 's landscapes with different issues and times creates invented landscapes , he says . <p> He often goes to places on a " painting holiday " , such as Kaka Point or Tolaga Bay , where he visits tourist sites , using them as a starting point for a series of works . <p> " People go , ' you must depend on these places for subject matter ' , but often I introduce other things to them . ' ' <p> In one Kaka Point work he put historic con-artist Amy Bock ( 1859-1947 ) in it as he had seen her mentioned in a tourist brochure at the hotel . <p> Seeing works from more than 40 years brought together for the exhibition at Milford Galleries has @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ before . <p> " It 's quite unusual for me because I 've been having all these shows year after year . I started in the early ' 70s . And they all go out . Until you see them all together , you realise that there are links between them all . And there 's common things that you keep . " <p> Brown likes variety in artwork but realises that no matter how hard he tries , he can not overcome his natural tendencies . <p> He acknowledges using words on painting is something a lot of people do not like . <p> " They say get rid of them . And I understand that . Because there are not many painters who can combine words on painting without them being a distraction . ' ' <p> Works in the exhibition include some of his favourites such as They Are Us ( 2019 ) about the Christchurch Massacre . ' ' <p> Triptych Listening to Ruru ( 2023 ) is another " complicated ' ' work on display . Henry intrigued him as his father had been a deer culler in the area . <p> " So he 's a conservation symbol in my work in a general way and the ruru can be a question of everything . ' ' <p> The work also features LGBTQ+ symbols reflecting his own support for the community of which his stepson Josh Thompson is part of . <p> The larger works are more satisfying to complete , he says . <p> " But I 've been surprised sometimes by pulling out the little works . @@138599524 <p> In 150 years , the Otago Art Society has changed and developed but at its heart still provides an important opportunity for local artists to exhibit and the community to see art , discovers Rebecca Fox . <p> There might be 90 years between Joan Hardey and Persephone Wells but they have one thing in common F- a love of art . <p> The pair love drawing and painting , although they are at very different stages of their journey . Hardey , at 100 , no longer paints and Persephone , 10 , is just beginning . <p> But both are members of the Otago Art Society ( OAS ) , the youngest and oldest members , and both of their works will be exhibited in the society 's 150th exhibition , celebrating New Zealand 's oldest art society . <p> President Rose Shepherd says the pair are a good example of what today 's art society has come to be - an inclusive , diverse society which welcomes everyone . <p> Gone are the days where an artist had to apply to join and have their art @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Instead , anyone who joins up is eligible to have their work exhibited . <p> There are also members like Hardey who may no longer make art but enjoy being involved in the society and support it in other ways . <p> Hardey , who was recognised for her contribution to the society with the naming of one of the gallery spaces after her two years ago , says it is meeting like-minded people that is one of the most important things about the society . <p> Otago Art Society president Rose Shepherd with one of her entries in the 150th exhibition . PHOTO : PETER MCINTOSH <p> She joined in the 1960s , after starting the Wakari Art Group with a friend in 1962 , enjoying the comraderie of the society and the workshops and events offered over the years . <p> " It 's just such a supportive group , you know , for new artists . ' ' <p> Back then the society was meeting in the Moran Rooms . <p> She remembers having open-air exhibitions near the Dunedin Library and Town Hall , each artist having @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ activity . " <p> But it was not long before member and president ( 1966-69 ) Shona Macfarlane proposed establishing a gallery at the old North Dunedin post office in 1968 ( now the HD Skinner Annex of Tuhura Otago Museum ) . <p> By the early 2000s , the art society had grown out of that space and it moved to the first floor of Dunedin Railway Station in 2007 . <p> As well as four gallery spaces and administration offices , it also had a large shop space . <p> Hardey , a vice-patron and life member , volunteered to open the shop on a Saturday and did so until she was " well into " her 90s . <p> " I landed doing it every Saturday , and loved it . So for about 16 years , I opened the gallery on a Saturday . ' ' <p> When she wanted to learn etching , the society held classes - and over the years , classes , workshops , demonstration days and lectures have been offered regularly . <p> " There 's always activities . I just @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ all these interesting people , both artists and supporters . " <p> Dunedin artist Lynn Taylor credits an OAS exhibition at the Skinner Annex in the 1980s for starting her journey in print making when she made her first art purchase , a print by member Ludmila Sakowskian . <p> " The graphic marks and way the paper was moulded with an embossing technique became the start of my journey into printmaking . " <p> She eventually studied printmaking through to masters level . <p> " I 've had a wonderful career inking up plates and printmaking all over the world . You never know when a moment viewing artwork will change your perception or life journey . ' ' <p> The development over the years of major exhibitions which attract artists from around New Zealand , including the biennial Hope and Sons Art Awards ( since 1998 ) and the Cleveland National Art Awards in 2017 , has also increased its importance to the art community . <p> Dunedin School of Art ( DSA ) principal lecturer Rachel Allan says the OAS plays a vital role in supporting DSA @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and develop their work in a professional setting . <p> A key example is the Cleveland award , which has consistently showcased the work of its students , graduates and lecturers . <p> " Through initiatives like this , the Otago Art Society fosters strong connections within the local arts community while championing new talent and artistic excellence . " <p> Juxtaposition by Nicole Kolig . PHOTO : PETER MCINTOSH <p> The school and society have been closely connected since their beginnings , Jim Tomlin noting in his history of the school that the DSA opened in 1871 and its first head of school , David Con Hutton , became the society 's first secretary on its founding in 1976 and later became vice-president . <p> Former DSA head Bridie Lonie says the retention of the skills that underpin the visual arts requires a broad base . <p> " The OAS in its inclusivity provides this broad base , thereby acting as one of the foundations of the arts in this city that must have more artists per capita than most . " <p> This year , the society 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 100 on previous years . <p> Shepherd says the society has lots of different , diverse members , in age , nationality and life histories . <p> For Taylor , the exhibitions also play an important role in community discourse about ideas and issues . <p> " I see exhibitions not just for aesthetic display but as a platform - an activated space where art is an agent for change . Exhibitions bring people together . Over the last 20 years , there has been an increase in community arts and science participation projects as a way to deal with issues such as climate change and scratch at complex issues in a collaborative way . Exhibiting the artistic results of such projects shares , communicates viewpoints , sparks innovation and solidifies communities . " <p> Rats in a Minefield by Cara Fotofili . PHOTO : PETER MCINTOSH <p> The 150th exhibition has attracted one of the highest number of entries at 220 , pushing exhibition designer Jenny Longstaff to her limits to find space for them all . <p> But it is exciting to see that so many people value @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> The community is also a strong supporter of the gallery , 130,000 people visiting the gallery annually . In the summer , the majority of visitors are from cruise ships and tourists but in the winter it is local visitors . <p> The recent heritage exhibition , featuring 40 artworks extracted from their storage place in the Dunedin Railway Station clock tower , attracted visitors from across the region . <p> They were keen to see the works , some on display for the first time in 50 years and had little provenance to them . <p> * Since the 1930s , a large collection of paintings have been donated to the society . <p> * Up until the 1970s , exhibitions were held in various venues around Dunedin including the Municipal Chambers , Public Art Gallery , Concert Chamber , Pioneer and Garrison Halls and part of the present Otago Settlers Museum . <p> * Committee meetings were held in diverse locations such as private homes , the Moran Building and the billiard saloon above the former Tip Top Milk Bar in the Octagon. 
@@138599602 <h> Chris Brandolino discusses El Nino outlook <p> El Nino is officially underway - and could develop into one of the strongest on record . <p> Earth Sciences New Zealand ( ESNZ ) has followed counterparts in Australia , Japan and the US in declaring an El Nino event , in its seasonal climate outlook released today . <p> It said the climate phenomenon would strongly influence New Zealand 's weather during spring and into summer , with " spiky " temperatures in some places . <p> The agency expected drier than usual conditions in the north and east , while some parts of the south and west South Island would likely be wetter than normal . <p> Principal scientist Chris Brandolino said conditions in the Pacific Ocean had now met ESNZ 's threshold , with the ocean and atmosphere now " coupled " - with higher than normal sea surface temperatures in the eastern Pacific and weaker trade winds that would normally drive rainfall towards New Zealand . <p> Brandolino said this year 's El Nino would , at the very least rival the intensity of the five strongest @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " There are compelling reasons to believe it will exceed that , and it has all the makings to be an historically strong event ... based on what we 're seeing in the observations in the ocean as well as the modelling . " <p> Summer rainfall patterns in New Zealand during two previous strong El Nino events <p> Supplied / ESNZ <p> That did not necessarily translate into equally profound effects on New Zealand but it did increase those odds , he said . <p> " When we have these strong or very strong so-called Super El Ninos , we tend to find stronger impacts or more profound impacts . " <p> There were also other climate drivers that affected New Zealand 's weather , but El Nino was currently at the steering wheel , he said . <p> That translated into drier , windier spring conditions across most of the North Island and along the east coast of the South Island . <p> Conversely , it historically meant more rain than usual for Southland and the lower half of the West Coast . <p> Exactly how that played @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " Every El Nino event has its own fingerprint , its own personality . " <p> The expected effects of El Nino during spring will vary around the country . <p> Supplied / ESNZ <p> Despite the long-range forecast , parts of the South Island would first have to contend with a significant rain event next week , which would " help take the edge off " the dry conditions to come . <p> " Were it not for this expected rain event for parts of the eastern South Island , boy , they would be in some real dire straits , " Brandolino said . <p> " Right now , they are in meteorological drought in parts of Canterbury ... To have drought in winter , that 's really remarkable . " <p> Current soil moisture levels were mostly normal in the North Island , he said . <p> " That will probably buy some time , but as we get deeper into the calendar year , the effects of increased wind , the effects of reduced rainfall , that will start to become more noticeable . " <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Nino event is underway . <p> Supplied / ESNZ <p> The current heatwaves in parts of Europe and the northern hemisphere were also linked in part to El Nino and the hotter Pacific Ocean temperatures . he said . <p> " We have heat from the ocean , we have heat in the atmosphere , and all that heat is getting redistributed across the globe . " <p> That was happening against a background of climate change-driven temperature increases - meaning the hottest days were getting hotter . <p> New Zealand , too , could expect some unusually warm days . <p> " We 're going to be seeing our own version of that - perhaps not 40 ? C or 44 ? C like we 've seen in Europe - but certainly that spikiness is something we 're expecting . " <p> The country was already seeing historically high temperatures for this time of year . <p> " In Bromley ... we set a new national June maximum temperature : 26 ? @@138599931 <h> What can we learn from whale poo ? <p> Caption:A southern right whale , or tohora , and its calf.Photo credit:Supplied / University of Auckland Tohora Research Team <p> A molecular ecologist is hoping studying the faeces of endangered southern right whales , or tohora , will help protect them from the threat of climate change . <p> Last year , ABC reported an Australian man 's unusual jam jar collection of frozen whale faeces had led to new information . <p> Rod Keogh had been collecting the faeces from South Australia 's Fowlers Bay for more than seven years . <p> His treasure trove had led scientists to learn about the diet and gut health of the tohora , which is hoped to help increase the population , as global warming and rising water temperatures could be putting the whale 's traditional diet at risk . <p> University of Auckland Associate Professor Emma Carroll told Checkpoint she had also studied faeces from New Zealand 's Auckland Islands and South Africa . <p> " It 's amazing what you can learn about whales by studying their poo . It @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ whale poo helps with scientific discovery <p> Carroll said tohora were historically thought of as another kind of humpback whale , which migrated to Antarctica and ate krill . <p> However , it turned out that tohora did not do that . <p> " They 're actually staying around the kind of mid-latitude , around what we call the subtropical convergence , and they 're eating more prey than we ever thought , " she said . <p> " This is really exciting . It links in with satellite telemetry work that we 've been doing on the species as a global community , that shows that these whales are actually travelling and feeding from , like , 30 degrees south , all the way to the ice age . <p> " So , in a changing ocean , they 're taking advantage of lots of different ecosystems around the southern hemisphere . " <p> Carroll said it was unlikely that their diet had changed , but scientists were only now able to understand more about their foraging ecology , as whaling had decimated the population . <p> " We @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " Not having reliance on one , or two , types of prey has to be good , when ecosystems are changing throughout the Southern Ocean . " <p> However , Carroll said it was still unknown whether their diet was nutritious enough . That was what she hoped to find out next . <p> Carroll also hoped to be able to study how the changing ecosystems would affect the tohora . <p> " Ninety percent of our whales are foraging in a region south of Australia , but , you know , we 're not able to sample their poo when they 're out in the open ocean like that , and , in fact , this region is virtually unstudied . We do n't really know what kind of ecosystem and plankton are there . " <p> However , she had hope for the future of the tohora . <p> " They 're able to adapt and they 've survived for thousands of years already . They 've survived different types of environmental change . <p> " I think if we can give them a chance , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and entanglement , then I think they can continue to recover from whaling . We 've supported their recovery once , from 400 to 15,000 , and I think we can continue . " 
@@138618133 <p> New Zealand is facing the prospect of one of the strongest El Ni ? o events on record , with Earth Sciences New Zealand confirming that El Ni ? o conditions have now been established and are expected to significantly shape the country 's weather through to 2027 . Scientists say warming sea surface temperatures across the central and eastern tropical Pacific ... <p> New Zealand is facing the prospect of one of the strongest El Ni ? o events on record , with Earth Sciences New Zealand confirming that El Ni ? o conditions have now been established and are expected to significantly shape the country 's weather through to 2027 . <p> Scientists say warming sea surface temperatures across the central and eastern tropical Pacific have reached the threshold for an El Ni ? o event and are continuing to strengthen . Current modelling suggests the event could rival , or even surpass , the most powerful El Ni ? o episodes recorded in 1972 , 1982 , 1991 , 1997 and 2015 . <p> For New Zealand , El Ni ? o typically brings stronger westerly @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Northern and eastern regions are expected to experience drier-than-normal conditions , while the western South Island is likely to see above-average rainfall . <p> The seasonal outlook indicates that Canterbury , Marlborough , eastern Otago , Wairarapa , Hawke 's Bay and Gisborne are among the regions most likely to become significantly drier as spring develops . Northland , Auckland , Bay of Plenty and Taranaki are also forecast to experience below-normal rainfall , while Southland and the lower West Coast can expect wetter conditions . Much of the country is also forecast to become windier from late winter into spring . <p> The forecast raises concerns for farmers , growers and rural communities . Reduced rainfall and persistent winds are expected to dry soils , reduce groundwater recharge and increase pressure on water supplies . Elevated temperatures combined with low humidity are also likely to heighten wildfire risk across many eastern and northern regions . An infographic included with the release identifies Canterbury , Marlborough , Wairarapa , Hawke 's Bay , Gisborne , Bay of Plenty and Northland as areas facing an increased wildfire threat . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ will still bring periods of unsettled weather . A significant rain event is expected during the first part of July before the longer-term El Ni ? o pattern becomes more dominant later in winter and into spring . <p> Scientists caution that while El Ni ? o provides a strong indication of seasonal trends , it is not the only influence on New Zealand 's climate . Other factors , including ocean temperatures around New Zealand , the Indian Ocean Dipole , Antarctic weather systems and the effects of climate change , will also shape conditions over the months ahead . <p> For Maori communities , particularly those with strong connections to whenua and primary industries , the forecast will be closely watched . Dry conditions can affect farming , horticulture , forestry and customary food gathering , while increased wildfire risk and pressure on freshwater resources may also impact rural marae and communities . <p> Earth Sciences New Zealand says it will continue monitoring the evolving event and provide updated seasonal outlooks as the El Ni ? o strengthens over the coming months . 
@@138630511 <p> Every few years , scientists and science journalists pick a day to patiently explain the El Ni ? o weather phenomenon to us , and every time we mostly understand . That day was yesterday , as Earth Sciences New Zealand ( ESNZ ) officially declared El Ni ? o a weather event here , reports Kate Newton for RNZ . <p> In short , El Ni ? o is a naturally-occurring phenomenon when trade winds ( east-to-west ) weaken , leading to unusually warm water in the eastern Pacific and cooler water in the far western Pacific . During El Ni ? o , New Zealand is likely to experience stronger and more frequent winds from the west in summer , leading to dryness in the eastern parts of the country and more rain in the west . <h> A super El Ni ? o and cause for concern <p> Speaking to Toby Manhire on the At Large podcast , ESNZ 's Jon Tunster said New Zealand is " well on track " to experience a " strong " El Ni ? o , with sea surface temperature @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ? o originates , already 1 ? C above average ( at 1.5 ? C it becomes a " strong " El Ni ? o ) . But Tunster anticipates even more increases , potentially to 2 ? C , or a " super " El Ni ? o . <p> How strong exactly ? In comparing this year 's to the five strongest El Ni ? o events in New Zealand history ( 1972 , 1982 , 1991 , 1997 and 2015 ) , principal scientist Chris Brandolino said " there are compelling reasons to believe it will exceed that , and it has all the makings to be an historically strong event . " <p> ESNZ 's forecasting suggests stronger winds and dry days for the east coasts during spring , bringing with it heightened risk of drought and wildfires heading into summer . This will be of particular concern for places like Christchurch , which just recorded its driest May on record , reports The Press ( paywalled ) . <p> In The Conversation in May , three scientists argued New Zealand has underestimated its vulnerability , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Droughts far more severe than those experienced in recent memory already exist within New Zealand 's climate history . Yet many modern risk assessments still rely heavily on relatively recent events , potentially underestimating the true scale of drought variability the country is capable of experiencing . " <p> One such " forgotten big dry " was in the summer of 1914-15 , also during El Ni ? o . But University of Waikato climate scientist Luke Harrington stressed to RNZ ( Newton again ) that not every El Ni ? o brings a drought and not every drought happens during El Ni ? o . <h> Is Europe 's melting summer headed here ? <p> Not quite . While some local reports this week have attributed Europe 's record-breaking temperatures of late to El Ni ? o , the World Weather Attribution ( WWA ) consortium analysed the heatwaves , particularly in relation to climate change impacts , and ruled out any influence from El Ni ? o , reported the Guardian last week . Instead , WWA scientists said the high pressure system creating a heat dome over @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it severely this year . <p> However , record-breaking temperatures in Europe and around the world , along with a warming Pacific ocean and unseasonably warm days already this winter , suggests New Zealand may see some very hot days this year . " Perhaps not 40 ? C or 44 ? C like we 've seen in Europe , but certainly that spikiness is something we 're expecting , " said Brandolino . <h> This is going to ruin the tour school holidays <p> While ESNZ 's forecasting for El Ni ? o is largely focussed on spring impacts , they 've noted a " significant rain event within the first 10 days of July " likely to impact places like Canterbury and Marlborough . His track record for stickability was questionable . <p> We tried to talk him out of it several times , but he was determined and , after passing aptitude and fitness tests , he was ready to embark on the introductory 16-week basic training at Waiouru Military Camp . For these 16 weeks the 160 recruits had their mobile phones confiscated and our only way of communicating with our son ( bar the odd good behaviour phone allowance on a Sunday evening ) was through old-fashioned letter writing . At times it felt like we had been thrust back in time to 1940 and he was on the frontline . <p> As an aside , the difference we noticed in our son after having no phone at his disposal was palpable . The handful of times we were @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ we were allowed to visit -- he was well-slept and more upbeat than we had seen him in some time . It was a real-time case study in how smartphones impact mental wellbeing in young people . Sadly , at the end of the 16 weeks they all got their phones back . <p> This 16-week training period was intensive , a monumental test of physical and mental fortitude , which about 10% of the recruits found too much ( I was amazed it was only 10% ) . But , it turns out our son loves sleeping outdoors in the Central Plateau in a self-dug trench filled with icy water in the middle of winter . Go figure . He made it to the end and not only did he make it , he by and large loved the experience . And it was an experience he was paid to have . <p> When he departed for basic training he was still a kid in many respects , but a mere 16 weeks later a fully-formed young man emerged . A young man who had way more respect and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to keep his room tidy , a young man who got on and did things rather than having to be reminded of them 10 times , a young man who was incredibly fit , and who was motivated . It seemed to us that these 16 weeks had fast-tracked the reconnection of his frontal lobe and we felt strangely indebted to the NZDF for this unexpected development . <p> Recruits at Waiouru Army Camp ( Photo : Supplied ) <p> Basic training was the first indication that maybe this was a good career path for our son , maybe we were wrong and maybe there was a good chance we were going to have to eat our words . <p> Watching him graduate from basic training was a very proud day . We had n't known quite what to expect but it turns out that watching a full military review with 150 young , uniformed soldiers performing drills and marching together in jaw-droppingly precise formation is a stunning spectacle to behold . Our boy was no longer a civilian , he was now a soldier . <p> Since completing basic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in Palmerston North , where he 's undertaking specialist training to become a signaller ; a communications specialist who is responsible for establishing and maintaining battlefield communications . By and large he is loving this path he has chosen and is showing no signs of wavering . He is now officially employed by the NZDF and is being paid a very decent wage for his age , while learning some highly-valuable skills . <p> The specialisation he has chosen is a frontline one , which does bring with it some fear for us . <h> More Reading <p> While at the end of 2024 it seemed highly likely the only war he would ever have to fight was the war against climate change , the mood of the world has since shifted significantly . When we have conversations about international conflict now it is very different knowing that he could one day be part of it . <p> We have learned so much about the NZDF through our son 's journey . We had no idea just how many career options are on offer ( pretty much any career you @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ zero potential to be anywhere near any action -- like carpentry , or dentistry , or Military Police Officer . <p> With so many young people graduating from university and unable to find employment -- a good portion with a sizeable student loan around their necks -- it feels increasingly like a viable career path that our son has chosen , and even a sensible one . And , while still remaining firmly anti-war in our beliefs we have most certainly had to endure the uncomfortable indigestion that comes from eating our words . <h> Members <h> Subscribe <h> FOLLOW US <h> DOWNLOAD THE APP <p> The Spinoff is subject to NZ Media Council procedures . @@138660421 <p> The government 's reasoning is that climate policy should be decided by elected officials and existing laws , not by the courts , to ensure " legal clarity and certainty " for businesses around their obligations . <p> Now the dispute has taken another turn . Smith has filed fresh proceedings in the High Court , asking for a declaration that the government 's decision to introduce the law change -- and the process behind it -- were unlawful . <p> There are many interesting aspects of these cases that legal observers will be following closely . Among them is whether New Zealand 's proposed law change is consistent with the international climate commitments it recently endorsed . <h> A landmark case <p> Smith alleges the companies he is suing have materially contributed to the climate crisis through their greenhouse gas emissions . He also argues their actions have caused harm to his whenua and moana , including places of cultural , spiritual and historical significance to him and his whanau . <p> In 2020 , the companies asked the courts to strike out Smith 's case before it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ courts before the Supreme Court heard the final appeal in 2022 . <p> In a landmark unanimous decision in 2024 , the Supreme Court ruled that while Smith 's claims -- including public nuisance , negligence and a novel tort of " climate system damage " -- were legally novel , they raised arguable questions that deserved to be tested at trial rather than dismissed at an early stage . <p> The court ordered the case be reinstated in full and proceed to the High Court , where it is currently scheduled for trial in April 2027 . <p> If the government 's amendment becomes law , however , it will bring an end to that trial before it begins -- and block any future lawsuits against corporate emitters . <p> That is relevant not just for Smith 's case , but for New Zealand 's wider climate obligations . <p> The resulting UN resolution was adopted by consensus in 2023 . The International Court of Justice delivered its advisory opinion in 2025 , unanimously finding that states have obligations to protect the climate system and that there may be @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Advisory opinions are not legally binding in the same way as judgements in contested cases . But opinions from the International Court of Justice are highly authoritative and are often cited by courts and tribunals around the world . <p> One important aspect of the court 's opinion concerned private actors . The court said states may be responsible if they fail to take necessary regulatory and legislative measures to limit emissions caused by private entities within their jurisdiction . <p> Seen in that light , removing an individual 's ability to sue companies over climate damage could be seen as weakening one possible " check and balance " on emissions caused by private actors . <h> A gap between words and action <p> The timing of the recent developments is also notable . Only eight days after announcing the proposed law change in May , New Zealand voted with 140 other countries at the UN to endorse the International Court of Justice 's advisory opinion on climate change . <p> New Zealand 's parliament also declared a climate emergency in 2020 . Yet domestically , the government is proposing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ could be held accountable for climate-related harm . <p> This does not mean every climate case against a company must succeed . That is for the courts to decide on the evidence and the law . But preventing such cases from being heard at all raises a different concern . <p> It risks placing New Zealand 's domestic climate policy out of step with the international climate principles it has supported . <p> Climate change is one of the defining challenges of this century . The science is clear that limiting global warming requires rapid and deep reductions in greenhouse gas emissions across all sectors this decade . <p> The proposed amendment is therefore about more than one lawsuit . <p> It raises fundamental questions about whether New Zealand 's laws will match the commitments it makes internationally -- and whether future generations will inherit climate policies that reflect accountability , or merely efforts to avoid it . <p> Your access to our unique and original content is free , and always has been . @@138671977 <h> Share this article <p> Reminder , this is a Premium article and requires a subscription to read . <p> A scene from NZIFF opening night film , Big Girls Do n't Cry . Supplied <h> 1 . Opening night <p> There 's a fine tradition of New Zealand International Film Festivals opening with great local films about the travails of adolescent girls , dating from when Heavenly Creatures made its local screen debut at the festival in 1994 . Since then have been 2004 's In My Father 's Den , and 2024'sWe Were Dangerous , and now stepping into the first-night spotlight is Big Girls Do n't Cry , the debut feature by director-writer Paloma Schneideman , the opening-night film in all 11 places the NZIFF is screening this year -- including Matakana , the area where it 's set , albeit 20 years ago . <p> Schneideman 's film is the queer-coming-of-age story of 14-year-old Sidney " Sid " Bookerman ( newcomer Ani Palmer ) set during the summer of 2006 when she finds herself drawn to a group of rich kids outside her usual circle , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ exchange student Freya ( Rain Spencer , The Summer I Turned Pretty ) . The cast also includes Aussie actor Noah Taylor as Sid 's father , a man who wants to be an artist but earns a crust mowing the lawns of the mansions on Omaha beach . The festival programme notes the film has echoes of Rain , Christine Jeffs ' 2001 debut feature which had its NZ premiere at the festival in 2001 and was another vivid , impressionistic tale of adolescent awakening by the seaside north of Auckland . <h> 2 . The local heroes <p> Last year , it was the Jacinda Ardern documentary Prime Minister which had the NZIFF as its home-country launchpad . This year , it 's another film about a New Zealander who became an international superstar -- Jonah Lomu . Lomu is the feature-length look at the All Blacks winger who became the biggest name in rugby as it entered the professional era in the 1990s , but who was felled by kidney disease and died in 2015 . It 's not the first documentary about him , but it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ widow Nadene Lomu making an unsuccessful legal challenge over trademark issues . <p> The film is an international production , co-directed by Irish film-maker Gavin Fitzgerald ( whose past doco subjects have included MMA fighter Conor McGregor and Oasis singer Liam Gallagher ) and Tongan-NZ director Vea Mafile'o , whose films Mother Tongue and For My Father 's Kingdom have shone a light on Tongan culture . Among those talking about Lomu 's life away from rugby are his brother John and mother Hepi , as well as former coaches and team mates . Conveniently , none of the Saturday NZIFF screenings of Lomu nationwide clash with any All Blacks fixtures . <p> As last year had Prime Minister , this year , there 's one about a deputy PM : Last Man Standing is a tribute to Jim Anderton , the one-time Labour Party president and Sydenham MP who stood in opposition to Rogernomics and led his breakaway New Labour into the Alliance Party . In the Labour-Alliance coalition elected in 1999 , he served as deputy to Helen Clark for one term . It 's produced by seasoned @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ deputy , Matt Robson , and directed by veteran documentarian Gerd Pohlmann. <h> 3 . The Cannes contenders <p> As has often been the case , the NZIFF has picked up many of the movies that made a splash at May 's Cannes Film Festival . It has 10 of the 22 films that were in the festival 's main competition -- and all of the winners of the major categories . <p> That includes Fjord , the Norwegian drama by Romanian director Cristian Mungiu , who is now one of 10 directors to have been twice awarded Cannes ' top prize , the Palme d'Or , having won in 2007 for his 4 Months , 3 Weeks and 2 Days . Fjord is a story of clashing ethics with Renate Reinsve and Sebastian Stan as a devout couple , newly arrived from Romania in a Norwegian fishing village , whose treatment of their kids attracts the attention of neighbours and care workers . <p> Also in the programme are the Cannes ' second and third place-getters -- Grand Prix winner Minotaur , a dark thriller by maverick Russian director @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the story of an archaeologist working in the Thrace region between Bulgaria , Greece and Turkey , by German director Valeska Grisebach . Also on offer are Fatherland , the historical drama starring Sandra H ? ller which won Polish film-maker Pawel Pawlikowski best director . And the festival includes the two films which the Cannes jury decided to award joint shared actor and actress prizes to -- Coward , the World War I queer love story by Belgian director Lukas Dhont , and All of a Sudden , the French care home drama by Japanese director Ryusuke Hamaguchi. <h> 4 . Arthouse veterans <p> The NZIFF gives audiences a chance to get reacquainted with auteurs who have been frequent flyers in past programmes . Among those returning are Jim Jarmusch with his Venice Golden Lion-winning Father Mother Sister Brother , which features a very Jarmusch cast of Tom Waits , Adam Driver , Charlotte Rampling , Cate Blanchett and Vicky Krieps . <p> Also back , at the age of 80 , is French director Claire Denis with The Fence -- an adaptation of a 1979 play by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ which returns Denis to another drama in West Africa where she spent much of her childhood . <p> Spanish institution Pedro Alm ? dovar returns with tragicomedy Bitter Christmas , a film that was in competition at Cannes . <p> With hostage thriller Dead Man 's Wire , American indie stalwart Gus Van Sant returns to the big screen for the first time since 2018 's Do n't Worry , He Wo n't Get Far on Foot with a cast that includes Al Pacino , Bill Skarsg ? rd , Dacre Montgomery and Colman Domingo . <h> 5 . Getting animated <p> This year 's festival offers a chance to see NZ 's first animated feature and its latest . Footrot Flats : The Dog 's Tale , the hit 1986 adaptation of Murray Ball 's cartoon strip that he co-wrote and directed -- and which did wonders for the career of one Dave Dobbyn who provided its songs -- is getting 40th anniversary screenings . Meanwhile , Kiri and Lou Go Raaa ! -- the feature extension of Harry Sinclair and Anthony Elworthy 's Bafta-nominated claymation kids ' series @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ its bow , complete with songs by Sinclair 's old Front Lawn offsider Don McGlashan . <p> From further afield , the animated offerings include the heftily titled French-Belgian Oscar-nominated Little Am ? lie or The Character Of Rain about life seen through the eyes of a 2 ? -year-old growing up in Japan in the 1960s . And also Oscar-nominated and showing a distinct Hayao Miyazaki influence is the French sci-fantasy adventure Arco , about a young boy living in the future who must find his way to his home in the sky . <h> 6 . The Music <p> For anyone who found themselves going down a deep well of Split Enz nostalgia via their recent reunion tour ( and the Listener 's cover story ) , here 's a little more on offer . Among the festival 's retrospective offerings is Nambassa Festival , the feature about the 1979 event which captures the band in full flight after the release of Frenzy -- the first album to include songs by recent recruit Neil Finn . <p> My Humble Friend , Fonoti Pati Umaga , a film about @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in an accident in 2005 , follows the play Music Portrait of a Humble Disabled Samoan , which told his story in arts festival seasons earlier this year . <p> For jazz fans , Everybody Digs Bill Evans is a biographical drama , based on the 2013 Owen Martell novel Intermission , about the dark period in the pianist 's career after the death of his trio 's bassist , Scott LaFaro . Evans is portrayed by Norwegian actor Anders Danielsen Lie and director Grant Gee was awarded Best Director at the Berlin International Film festival . <p> And for those who admire anyone who can hold a tune with just their lips , Whistle is an Australian-made doco about the global stars of competitive whistling . It 's really a thing . Best leave the dog at home , though . <p> Split Enz at the Nambassa Festival in 1979 . Wikipedia commons . <h> 7 . The natural world <p> American documentary-makerSara Dosa 's 2022 vulcanologist movie Fire of Love was one of the great films of that year 's festival . She returns with something cooler -- @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the 700-year-old Okj ? kull glacier in Iceland . It 's based on the writings of Andri Sn ? r Magnason , whose family have observed the country 's glaciers for generations . Dosa 's film mixes family archives with contemporary footage as it looks at how climate change is altering the Icelandic landscape . <p> By the looks of Whispers in the Woods , wildlife photographer Vincent Munier has produced another visually wondrous natural history film to follow his 2021 Velvet Queen , in which he went in search of the Tibetan Snow Leopard . The " woods " are the forests of Vosges , in eastern France , where the local fauna includes bears , deer , owls , and lynxes . <h> 8 . The Japan collection <p> The international component of this year 's programme looks to be dominated by Europe in various co-production confederations . But the line-up has a strong thread of Japanese films of various genres . Among them is Sheep in the Box , the latest by arguably Japan 's greatest living director , Hirokazu Kore-eda , which is both in familiar @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ -- and a foray into sci-fi . In the near future , two grieving parents turn to a tech company providing the bereaved with humanoid replications of their dead loved ones . The couple get more time with their 7-year-old son Kakeru , until the humanoid boy starts developing beyond being a replicant . <p> Elsewhere , the Japanese offerings include a period samurai thriller ( The Samurai and The Prisoner ) , contemporary romance ( All the Lovers in the Night ) , and deadpan workplace comedy ( AnyMart ) . <h> 9 . Aramoana revisited <p> Last year 's great Pike River movie reminded us that director Robert Sarkies has wrestled valiantly with our country 's darkest moments a couple of times before . In 2006 , he delivered Out of the Blue , his unnerving account of the 1990 Aramoana massacre in which local resident David Gray killed 13 people , most of them his neighbours in the coastal village north of Dunedin . @@138696925 <p> Comment : A recent run of media stories has focused on young people 's despondency about the future . In one story , a young woman talked about the hopelessness felt by her generation , not having clear career pathways , struggling to put food on the table , and distress about climate change . She linked these problems to the mental health crisis and called for more government funding to support mental health . <p> Her comments highlight another problem : we have entered an era where the vocabulary of mental health is used to highlight and propose solutions to most of our problems . <p> But if unemployment and climate change are causing anxiety for young people , is it better to spend money on mental health ? Or on climate change , job creation , and the cost of living ? If we focus on the source of worry ( e.g. the warming climate ) , rather than on worry itself , we find different potential solutions and different outcomes . <h> read more <p> The Danish psychologist Svend Brinkmann hit the nail on the head @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . We can talk about our troubles in many different ways , he wrote , and the way we choose will determine not only the vocabularies available , but also the explanations and potential solutions . <p> In other words , it matters -- and matters deeply -- whether the distress felt by people is talked about as a psychological , moral , existential , or political problem . <h> Unintended outcome <p> The mental health awareness movement has helped teach us to look out for ourselves and one another , and that it 's okay to ask for help . But there 's also been an unintended outcome . Rather than just being aware of the potential to support mental illness , we 've begun to translate other problems into mental health ones . <p> The go-to position is to default to a psychological language and its associated treatments for what ails us . Normal emotional responses to injustice , inequity , and abandonment are seen as some sort of psychological disorder . <p> Rather than seeing this generation as slowly melting snowflakes in need of " tools " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be funded as fully as it was for most of the politicians who have pulled up the ladder behind them . <p> This " pathologisation " -- the treatment of something normal as abnormal -- is linked by its logic to a particular remedy ( counselling , or psychological and psychiatric intervention ) , which overloads a slender system designed to look after serious psychological disorders . It 's also the wrong strategy . <p> By psychologising what is arguably a normal response to an abnormal situation , ' mental health ' becomes an **36;1305;TOOLONG approach . We attend to the impact of a social problem , rather than to the problem itself . <h> Talking about problems differently <p> It would make much more sense to talk about our problems using other vocabularies . <p> Brinkmann uses the case of the " work stress epidemic " to illustrate the difference between talking about problems with the language of mental health versus the language of politics . <p> Discussing work stress as a mental health problem can lead us down a path of offering individual employees access to counselling services @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the boss is asking you to work unreasonable hours ? Here 's a freephone number to talk to a therapist . <p> Brinkmann points out we could instead talk about unreasonable workplace conditions using " a political language of rights and duties , social justice and injustice " . When people are treated unjustly at work or anywhere else , expressing disapproval in political language is a valid response , he says . <p> And this , he argues , was what used to happen : " Detrimental work conditions were once something to be dealt with politically and collectively -- centred on the work of unions . " <p> The same political language of rights and duties , justice and injustice could be applied to any one of a number of problems we face in Aotearoa . <p> Using a political language rather than a mental health one , we could look at the pressures facing university students . Referred to as the " anxious generation " with growing concerns for their stress levels and psychological wellbeing , we support them to develop tools for " resilience " and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ their " top two inches " to overcome what are actually structural and economic constraints . <p> What used to be a free education system now costs thousands of dollars a year . This accompanies increased costs of rent , food , and heating . So , students rush from study to university to work , trying to make instant noodles feel like a meal and worrying about the accumulation of debt they will carry into a job market that is increasingly restricted . The mental health focus makes it plainly their problem . Develop your tools ! No wonder they are under pressure . <p> But what if we deployed a political language instead ? <p> Rather than seeing this generation as slowly melting snowflakes in need of " tools " , an argument could be mounted that university education should be funded as fully as it was for most of the politicians who have pulled up the ladder behind them . And that the young people of today should be given the same educational opportunities as these decision-making MPs enjoyed to be the leaders of tomorrow . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , a political argument would point out the inter-generational inequity resulting from ever-rising tertiary fees and haggle for redistribution of opportunities . It would also raise wider political questions about how we fund tertiary education and whether this is best done through general taxes or student fees . <p> When we default to the language of mental health to find solutions to our problems , we risk missing the chance to debate these wider political questions and to find an array of potential solutions , rather than just a sticking-plaster solution for each individual . <p> Importantly , and we must n't forget this either , by leaving the language of mental health for problems for which psychological or psychiatric help is the correct solution , we acknowledge that other issues are not " in our heads " or the problem of a sensitive younger generation . They are social , political , and structural problems that concern us all . <p> Annemarie Jutel is a professor emerita in the School of Health at Te Herenga Waka -- Victoria University of Wellington . She is a sociologist of diagnosis and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ... More by Annemarie Jutel 
@@138709407 <h> It 's not the weather : it 's the climate making it hot <p> A woman stands in a pile of ice to cool off at The Great American State Fair on the National Mall amid a heatwave in Washington , DC . PHOTO : REUTERS <p> Last week it was Europe 's turn , with a slow-moving ' ' heat dome ' ' that gave almost everybody from the United Kingdom to Poland three consecutive days of record-breaking temperatures . <p> In Germany it hit 41 ? C. That 's 106 ? Fahrenheit , if you live in the one country that still clings to British Imperial measures . ( Hint : it 's not Britain . ) <p> This week it 's the turn of the United States as a similar heat dome inflicts comparable temperatures on almost everywhere east of the Mississippi River . <p> Even central Canada is heading for temperatures in the mid-30s . Meanwhile the next heat dome has already arrived over Spain and Portugal , heading north and east to envelop the rest of the continent . <p> It 's not just @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Bangladesh and Pakistan have been closed much of the time since late April-early May because of extreme heat . Some pupils are set to lose six to eight weeks of classes . <p> You would almost think that there is some sort of global phenomenon driving these widespread , record-breaking heatwaves . <p> Well , I checked out that possibility , and you 'll be pleased to hear that nothing untoward is happening . It 's just random heat domes wandering past . <p> I looked it up on Fox Weather , and in a 800-word report on the record heat in the eastern United States there was n't a single mention of climate change . In fact , even the word ' ' climate ' ' did not appear . <p> Just to be sure , I also checked the coverage in a country whose leader is not Climate Change Denier-in-Chief . Britain 's Guardian newspaper is pretty reliable ( centre-left but evidence-based ) and today 's piece on the heatwave in North America did mention climate change -- once , in the last paragraph . <p> There is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ what is happening right now , and what that may portend for the not-very-distant future . <p> It 's complicated , it 's a bit frightening , and we all know that dealing with it would change our lives in unwelcome ways . ( So would not dealing with it , but that bill arrives a little later . ) <p> But if you want to know the current state of play , from someone who is not a climate scientist but has been heavily engaged with the topic for 15 years ( at least a hundred interviews with climate scientists ) , here it is , almost in bullet form . <p> Most people understand that we have to reduce global emissions of greenhouse gases fast or we are in deep trouble in the long term ( 20-30 years ) . <p> However , that is no longer enough , because the heating is happening much faster than predicted . The average global temperature they were expecting us to reach around 2035 is actually here now . <p> This accelerated warming risks setting off feedbacks that trigger even faster @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ' tipping points ' ' ; it 's the same phenomenon . ) <p> Scientists have a list of the feedbacks that will be triggered at various temperatures , some of which are already quite close : loss of summer sea ice in the Arctic , for example . <p> But the same scientists now fear that there are feedbacks waiting out there that we do n't know about . They even suspect that most of them will have to do with ocean currents and how they might shift as warming progresses . Indeed , in June 2023 we actually found one -- or rather , it found us . <p> In that one month the average global temperature leapt by almost one-third of a degree Celsius -- and it has never fallen back . The new normal is one and a-half degrees Celsius ' ' above pre-industrial ' ' ( +1.5 ? C ) , although we were hoping not to reach that level until 2035-40 . <p> The safest assumption now is that there are probably other unknown feedbacks out there , waiting to be triggered by some @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cut our emissions that fast ( and we ca n't ) , then we will have to hold the heat down artificially in the meantime . <p> That is still a hugely controversial subject in some quarters , but there is now a strong push to start doing small-scale experiments in the open air . We need to learn quickly if we can safely hold the temperature down by means like reflecting a tiny portion of the incoming sunlight back into space . <p> The short-term risk now is to avoid setting off a cascade of feedbacks that might quickly deliver us into really bad territory . That can only be achieved by holding the heat down . <p> In the long run we have to deal with the carbon dioxide and the methane and the various lesser emissions that are actually causing warming , but you ca n't get to the long run if you do n't survive the short run .

@@138733156 <h> Amid proposed changes to conservation law , can Florida serve as a cautionary tale for NZ ? <p> Unchecked commercial interests have left wild places in their state in poor shape , say Florida conservationists . " Florida has always prioritised economic development , " she says , describing decades of legal battles she fought on behalf of conservation interests against developers . <p> " Our laws , our society and our politics are focused on the next 10,000 people moving in , " continues Blackner . " In Florida , we have government of , by and for the developer . " <p> Florida is one of America 's most biodiverse regions , supporting panthers , bears , alligators and more than 500 species of birds across 80 unique ecosystems . Still , even the most optimistic conservationists admit that unchecked commercial interests have left wild places in poor shape . <p> Urban @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ National Park in Miami Dade Country , Florida ( Photo : Joe Raedle/Getty Images ) <p> From 1950 , Florida lost over 44% of its historic wetlands to urban development and agriculture ; over half the state 's vertebrates are experiencing population decline . <p> Wetland loss and industrial mismanagement have directly impacted people , too . The state 's drinking water is fed by aquifers that are under strain thanks , in part , to overloaded septic systems and urban runoff . <p> Deprived of areas that naturally absorb and control excess water , Florida 's low-lying urban centres are now at greater risk from climate change . Hurricanes , floods and rising sea levels pose more risk to Floridians than they ever have before . <p> Veteran conservationist Clay Henderson says weak foundational laws protecting biodiversity have made an uphill battle for those hoping to save what 's left of their wild places . @@138763229 <h> New Zealand 's tourism sector is striving to be greener . What does that mean for businesses ? <p> When choosing where to holiday , more of us than ever are thinking about our environmental footprint . <p> One recent survey found nearly three quarters of travellers considered sustainable travel important . A similar UK survey found 71% of travellers felt the same , with nearly half reporting that climate change influenced their choices . <p> For eco-conscious travellers , sustainability certifications make it simple to book activities and accommodation they can feel at ease about . Behind the scenes , however , things are n't so straightforward . <p> Vague definitions mean firms are left to work out what sustainable tourism actually means . Abstract concepts such as " net zero " and " regenerative tourism " often add to the confusion . Then comes the challenge of putting these into practice . <p> How should firms choose sustainability initiatives , what do these involve , and how do they keep their credentials ? <p> With the industry aiming to double the value of tourism exports within a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ social impacts -- this green goal is only going to become more important . <h> Costly , complex , but worthwhile <p> Among individual companies , we found that growing greener is more of an ongoing journey than a simple destination . <p> For many , taking the first step is difficult enough . Several managers explained they felt overwhelmed and could n't figure out where to start , often because they were n't sure where they were heading . <p> One manager we spoke with summed up this uncertainty when describing a local council 's goal of becoming carbon-zero by 2030 : " Does that mean the electricity ? Does it mean the transport ? Tell us what the hell that means . " <p> Other firms told us how sustainability initiatives that initially seemed simple proved to be anything but . <p> Recycling , for instance , was widely regarded as an easy place to start . But firms found it surprisingly difficult to manage because of seasonal fluctuations in waste , and inconsistent sorting by customers . <p> Some eventually hit roadblocks , forcing them to rethink @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ them . <p> Such stories highlight that , while worthwhile , becoming more sustainable can be time-consuming and costly . As one operator put it : " You ca n't be green if you 're in the red . " <p> But those early setbacks were rarely the end of the story . For many firms , they became the foundation for broader and more ambitious sustainability efforts . <p> Sustainability initiatives , for instance , tended to build on one another . Rather than ticking off a checklist , firms often found one change naturally led to the next . <p> Firms ' composting practices illustrated this . Food scraps became compost , which was then used to restore native vegetation around properties . As wildlife returned , some firms introduced predator control and biodiversity monitoring to protect it . <p> This pattern was common across the businesses we studied . As firms gained confidence and experience , their ambitions often expanded . <h> A moving target <p> No two firms we studied defined sustainability in exactly the same way or pursued the same mix of initiatives . And @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ businesses could truly be considered " sustainable " . <p> Some firms were frustrated by what they saw as constantly changing expectations -- be that trying to become " carbon neutral " , " net zero " or " nature positive " . Others viewed this as an ongoing journey , arguing firms could always be more sustainable . <p> Indeed , one of biggest takeaways from our research is that sustainable tourism is never really " finished " . As firms gain experience , one initiative often leads naturally to the next , creating an ongoing cycle of learning and improvement . <p> Firms can build momentum by starting with what they can control . Tracking energy use , water consumption and waste can help identify practical first steps . <p> Initiatives that require little effort from customers can also deliver early wins , giving firms the confidence to tackle more difficult sustainability challenges . <p> Firms can also make their progress more durable . Embedding sustainability into standard operating procedures , job descriptions and staff responsibilities can help ensure good practices continue , even as employees and leaders @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ role by creating neutral spaces where firms can share knowledge and learn from one another . <p> Helping businesses avoid common mistakes can reduce the time and cost involved in experimenting with new sustainability initiatives . <p> Ultimately , sustainable tourism is unlikely to be achieved by individual businesses acting alone . Progress will depend on continued collaboration between tourism operators , industry bodies , communities and visitors themselves . <p> Your access to our unique and original content is free , and always has been . Coastal communities , food production , freshwater supplies and biodiversity are all under increasing pressure as extreme weather events become more frequent and severe . <p> Professor Sandy Morrison ( Ngati Rarua , Ngati Maniapoto @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Indigenous Studies at the University of Waikato , says the report is another reminder that climate change is no longer a future challenge but a present-day reality demanding urgent action . <p> Professor Morrison believes Indigenous communities must play a much stronger role in shaping climate adaptation and resilience strategies . Maori and other Indigenous peoples have generations of environmental knowledge grounded in close relationships with whenua , moana and natural ecosystems , offering practical approaches to living sustainably with changing environmental conditions . <p> Rather than treating Indigenous knowledge as separate from science , there is growing recognition that combining matauranga Maori with scientific research produces more comprehensive and locally relevant climate solutions . This collaborative approach is helping communities better understand environmental change while developing adaptation strategies that reflect both cultural values and scientific evidence . <p> Examples are already emerging across Aotearoa and the Pacific , where iwi , hapu and Indigenous communities are working alongside researchers to restore wetlands , strengthen coastal ecosystems , monitor environmental change and improve long-term planning for climate resilience . These partnerships are contributing to stronger decision-making while ensuring local @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ continue developing climate adaptation policies , the WMO report highlights the importance of ensuring Indigenous voices are not simply consulted but actively involved in designing and leading responses . Recognising matauranga Maori alongside scientific knowledge offers an opportunity to build stronger , more resilient communities capable of responding to the increasingly complex challenges of a changing climate . <p> With the South-West Pacific experiencing some of the fastest environmental changes on the planet , the report reinforces that effective climate action will require collaboration across governments , scientists , communities and Indigenous leaders if the region is to successfully adapt to the challenges ahead .

@@138794578 <h> Report warns of soaring infrastructure flood costs <p> Damage and repair costs from inland flooding are expected to rise by up to 53 percent by 2075 , with some regions worse hit than others , a new report shows . <p> The cost of coastal flooding is currently lower , but is expected to rise faster - more than doubling under higher-emissions scenarios . <p> Following a five-year project that last year mapped flood risk across the country , the Earth Sciences study on behalf of the Infrastructure Commission used valuation data from the commission to model the increased costs of flooding as a result of climate change . <p> It showed that under 2025 conditions , inland flooding costs were estimated at an average $471.9 million a year - which included private buildings ( $190.8m ) , water infrastructure ( $68.4m ) , local roads ( $46.9m ) and central roads ( $25.3m ) . <p> That cost would rise by 45 percent - to about $682m a year - by 2075 under a medium greenhouse gas emissions scenario ; by 50 percent to $707.2m under " high @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ under a " very high " scenario . <p> Damaging floods do not hit every city or region every year , and the report warned this meant losses in a given year " may deviate substantially " from the annual average . <p> Coastal flooding losses - driven by sea level rise - would take longer to arrive but at an accelerating rate - going from $242.7m a year to $461.7m , $503.6m and $542.7m under the different emissions scenarios . <p> Infrastructure Commission economics and research director Graham Campbell welcomed the report , saying decisions made today would shape New Zealand for decades . <p> " While we can not predict exactly how the future will unfold , scenarios help us consider a range of plausible futures , understand the implications of different choices , and build infrastructure that is more resilient to uncertainty . " <p> The effects would not be felt evenly across the country . <p> In 2025 , Auckland topped the tables for highest average annual losses at $101.7m , followed by Christchurch ( $65.1m ) , Palmerston North ( $39.5m ) , Napier ( @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Palmerston North City stands out for its relatively high losses in education and government infrastructure , suggesting a concentration of public assets in hazard areas , " the report said . <p> Broken down by type of infrastructure , rail infrastructure was most at risk . <p> Annual rail losses from inland flooding in 2025 amounted to 0.31 percent of total network value , rising to 0.55 percent a year by 2075 under the very-high emissions scenario . <p> This was followed by water infrastructure ( from 0.21 percent to 0.36 percent ) , electricity networks ( 0.20 percent to 0.34 percent ) and local roads ( 0.16 to 0.27 ) . <p> The emissions scenarios used were Shared Socioeconomic Pathways or SSPs , an internationally recognised metric . The medium scenario estimates global temperature rise of about 2.7C above pre-industrial levels by 2100 , high represents a 3.6C increase , and very high a 4.4C increase . <p> To compare apples with apples , the modelling assumed assets would remain as they are , and did not account for potential future investments . <h> ' Resilience coming to the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ New Zealand 's infrastruture deficiencies will only grow - particularly for the regions facing the impact of climate damage on a regular basis . <p> Director of economics and research Graham Campbell told Morning Report , " The issue of resilience is coming to the fore . " <p> By 2075 under a high-emissions scenario , the areas expected to have the biggest increases in losses were Hauraki , Nelson City , Buller , Whakatane , and Napier . <p> Campbell said the report was the first step in understanding how risk would grow over time . <p> Resilience funding was competing for money against other infrastructure needs . <p> He hoped that next there would be research into indirect losses , like social impact .
