@@139037748 <p> Social media can expose young people to harm , but it can also provide enjoyment , connection , support and access to information , write Terryann Clark and Sarah Hetrick . Photo : Getty Images <p> Expert opinion from University of Auckland - Waipapa Taumata Rau <p> In his book The Anxious Generation , psychologist and author Jonathan Haidt made a case for mobile phones and social media causing an epidemic of mental illness . The book resonated widely , particularly with parents , and still does . Popular culture has reinforced these fears , with the Netflix series Adolescence vividly portraying the potential dangers of online life . <p> But the claim that social media is the primary driver of rising youth mental health problems does n't stack up . There is good evidence that indicators of poor mental health among young people were already rising before the mass introduction of social media , intersecting with increasing globalisation , unemployment , housing and food insecurity , widening inequity , discrimination , political polarisation , war and climate change . To imply that social media is the cause of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , economic and political environments of our children is disingenuous . <p> To be clear , there is currently no clear causal evidence linking social media to poor mental health among young people . Much of the research is cross-sectional , meaning it can identify associations but can not show that social media causes mental health problems . Studies that have found negative effects typically show that these are small , and follow-up research has often produced mixed or contradictory results . <h> read more <p> Take one of the most frequently cited studies , by psychologist Jean Twenge and colleagues . It found a link between social media use and depressive symptoms , but the relationship accounted for less than 1 percent of the variation in those symptoms . In other words , more than 99 percent of the differences in young people 's mental health were explained by other factors . <p> There is no doubt that we should be concerned about certain aspects of social media , including the wider digital environment and AI . Recent lawsuits in the United States and Mexico found major platforms designed @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . We agree that digital platforms must be held accountable for their content , with appropriate legislative protections . How we in Aotearoa and other nations respond to these concerns and enforce protections is hotly contested . <p> Australia 's decision to ban social media for under-16-year-olds from December 2025 has gone from a ' watch-with-interest ' case , to a global reference point in 2026 . A number of countries and states internationally are drawing up plans for a similar social media ban for young people , including France , Denmark , Greece , Austria , other EU member states and the UK . The US does n't have a federal ban , but more than 40 states have considered **25;2532;TOOLONG legislation this year . <p> A recent New Zealand select committee inquiry into online harms concluded that social media needs much stronger oversight . Its recommendations included tighter accountability for online harm , an independent regulator , bans on ' nudify ' apps and deepfakes , greater transparency around algorithms , restrictions on advertising , and more investment in education and research . It also backed a social @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ make good sense . Except the last one . <p> We do not support the under-16-year-old social media ban , although we can understand its appeal . Parents quite rightly worry about what their children are exposed to online . Yet banning total access may not produce the benefits many hope for . From a mental health perspective , most young people go online for help first -- they seek information , they look up services , they seek support and they like to hear other people 's experiences . Many health professionals are ' reaching in ' to engage with young people on social media , many of whom might never have asked for help elsewhere . <p> Most concerning , a ban on social media might inadvertently push young people who are suicidal or being targeted online away from their parents ( or other helping adults ) because they are afraid that they will get into trouble for being on social media . Early evidence from Australia after it introduced a ban found some parents reported improvements in family relationships and more face-to-face interaction . But around a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ lost access to peer support networks . A similar proportion reported their children migrating to less-regulated ( more dangerous ) corners of the internet instead . <p> Young people themselves have raised similar concerns about a social media ban , that what was designed to protect them could end up increasing isolation and vulnerability for some of those who need connection most . We are concerned that this could disproportionately affect Maori who already have poor access to support . They want to be , and should be , involved in designing solutions that ensure regulation of social media platforms that effectively address potential harms but ensure access to the benefits . <p> Why then , are governments around the world following Australia 's lead , even as evidence from Australia points to potential , albeit unintentional , adverse outcomes ? <p> To come up with the best solutions , we need to move beyond the binary concept , of social media being either good or bad . The impact of social media ( the good , bad and ugly ) depends on a range of factors , including how @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ content they engage with , their personal circumstances , their digital savviness , and whether screen time comes at the expense of sleep , exercise or face-to-face relationships . Social media can expose young people to harm . It can also provide enjoyment , connection , support and access to information . <p> This matters because social media is not simply a source of risk . For many young people it is also a source of friendship , community and support . It helps them keep relationships , explore who they are , find information and connect with others who share their experiences . For those who feel disconnected or marginalised in their offline lives , online spaces can provide a sense of belonging that is difficult to find elsewhere . <p> The challenge , then , is not simply how to keep young people off social media , given we know they are already by-passing the age of the ban for under-13s , but how to make their online environments safer while preserving the fun , connections and support that many rely on . <p> Policy makers want to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for young people and the evidence of potential harm , but require real solutions based on evidence , not hype . Banning social media for under 16-year-olds is a blunt instrument , a scapegoat for avoiding more complex social , political and economic problems causing distress among our children , while at the same time not holding social media giants to account and updating legislative protections for all New Zealanders . We urge careful consideration and an examination of the evidence to preserve the mental health and rights of young people . <p> Terryann Clark ( Ngapuhi , Ngati Wai , Ngati Whatua ki Kaipara ) is the Cure Kids professorial chair in child and adolescent mental health based in the School of Nursing , University of Auckland . More by Dr Terryann Clark

@@139054452 <h> Scientific voyage maps deep ocean changes across Cook Islands EEZ <p> A month long expedition underway in the Cook Islands is looking to further understand how climate change affects the ocean . <p> The voyage , which is a joint initiative between Climate Change Cook Islands and Earth Science New Zealand , aims to establish the first comprehensive baseline of conditions across the Cook Islands Exclusive Economic Zone ( EEZ ) . <p> Researchers aboard New Zealand 's state-of-the-art ocean research vessel Kaharoa II are collecting water samples , measuring ocean temperatures and currents , mapping the seafloor and testing for microplastics . <p> Voyage lead and oceanographer Denise Fernandez from Earth Science New Zealand said the expedition is the first step in building a long-term picture of the state of the Cook Islands ocean . <p> " Basically , we are trying to gather information from the ocean to describe the state of the ocean of the Cook Islands exclusive economic zone , so we can , in the future , know whether changes have been happening in this ocean . We can always come back to this @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ collecting data from the surface to depths of more than five thousand meters , with a particular focus on the Northern Cook Islands where ' ocean heat content ' has increased significantly . <p> Fernandez said this study is vital as the changes happening below the surface , directly affects local livelihoods . <p> " If climate change will have some impacts in the heat and the salinity and the currents - that will also cascade on affecting ecosystems in this area . <p> " We know that from Cook Islands ecosystems and supporting the food chains , is very important for many industries and for people , so we are trying to try to give a description of this area in this time . " <h> Predicting ocean changes <p> " We do n't know ourselves with 100 percent accuracy what is in our ocean . We generally know , of course there 's fish and there 's whales , and in some parts of it there 's manganese nodules . But what we do n't know is what else is there , if anything . " <p> " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ baseline . That 's all the information from the surface of the ocean right to the very bottom , and that will that will allow us to then know what changes will occur to that ocean over time . " <p> A bongo net sample . <p> Supplied / Kas Silk <p> For King , the information will help the Cook Islands understand how climate change is affecting fisheries , coral reefs , ocean chemistry and carbon storage - which he added , directly impacts the livelihoods of everyday Cook Islanders . <p> " That will be valuable to us as a country to better understand because we are an ocean state . Very small country land wise , very big country ocean wise . So we need to understand if there is change in those oceans . " <p> Fernandez said the data can be used to predict changes in the ocean , long before it happens . <p> " All of the information that we collect now to state how much the ocean is changing , how much heat the ocean can receive , it is helping models @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ years . <p> " For a model to perform well , it needs a baseline , it needs the real observation to understand the processes that happen in the ocean . The more information we gather , the more information we can provide to those models , the more we can know about the future . " <p> She added these predictions will support not only the Cook Islands resilience efforts , but those of the whole region . <p> " Because the ocean does n't have hard boundaries , so what happens in Cook Islands can expand and move somewhere else . So this is important that we do now , not only for the benefit of Cook Islands , but also for the South Pacific community . " <p> Kas Silk looking through the microscope one of the bongo net catches . <p> Supplied / Eleanor Haigh <h> Empowering local youth <p> On board as an observer is Tangimetua ' Tango ' Paerau from youth and environmental NGO Korero O Te Orau . <p> While he described the voyage as " outside his comfort zone " , he @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in scientific expeditions carried out in their waters - even if it is research they are unfamiliar with . <p> " This sort of stuff is way out of my comfort zone . @@139082595 <h> -- Advertisement -- <h> The Pacific 's Stake In Shaping AI 's Future -- UNDP <p> Last week , governments from across the world gathered in Geneva for the first session of the UN Global Dialogue on AI Governance , and a presentation of the inaugural report of the Independent International Scientific Panel on AI . It marked an important step towards building global rules for artificial intelligence . <p> While unpacking what AI can do for the Pacific , let us also consider what the Pacific can contribute to the future of AI . Both questions are relevant and connected , especially from a human development progress perspective . <p> The initial instinct , even anxiety , in small , remote economies could be to treat AI purely as a race from behind . And there is indeed need for that catch-up . So , the Kingdom of Tonga , for example , has moved through a national Digital Readiness Assessment , brought commercial 5G online , and is rebuilding its government services around a digital-first model , including AI tools designed to help citizens interact with the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Similar Digital Readiness Assessments have also been undertaken across Fiji , Samoa and Vanuatu , helping lay the foundations for AI adoption and digital transformation for the Pacific . This reflects strong national leadership and commitment across the government , with a willingness from the very top to jump into the race and speed up . <p> Foundational groundwork like this gives countries like Tonga real standing to speak on this issue from experience rather than aspiration . That includes investing in the AI skills and digital capabilities of people , to make the best use of the new data and services AI can offer . <p> However , framing the Pacific 's AI challenge only as a catch-up exercise misses what the region is better placed to see than almost anywhere else . In Tonga , as across the Pacific , AI systems run into something they were not designed to encounter . Much of what local communities know about maritime navigation , weather , land and the ocean is oral , relational , and held collectively , passed down through practice and inter-generational responsibility rather than stored @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and deliberately never written down . <p> The Pasifika Futures Report argues that the real question is not whether Pacific countries adopt AI , but whether technology supports the futures Pacific people want for themselves , especially younger generations . Digital Tuvalu offers perhaps the clearest example : a nation on the frontline of climate change using digital technology to preserve its culture , heritage and identity for generations to come , while advancing Simon Kofe 's vision of a future in which Tuvaluans can connect with one another , explore their ancestry and culture , and access new opportunities for business and commerce . <p> This is not a barrier to AI adoption . It is an opportunity to shape how these technologies are designed and governed . Tonga is therefore well placed to bring this Pacific perspective to the future of AI , contributing views on identity , stewardship , culture and knowledge systems that remain largely absent from today 's models and debates . <p> This brings up a larger missing piece in the current AI sphere -- experiential and non-written knowledge and decisions , as well @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ considerations . They are critical inputs that remain largely absent from global AI governance discussions today . Ask the AI frameworks and models how they would treat a body of knowledge whose custodians choose not to digitise it , and the answer is that they were not built with this in mind . Not yet . <p> Tonga 's own digital transformation gives it credibility to make this case precisely because it has not rushed . The Lagatoi Declaration , signed by Pacific ICT ministers in Port Moresby in 2023 , set out a shared regional approach to digital transformation , infrastructure and governance long before AI dominated the conversation . Tonga 's participation reflected a broader regional commitment : that a digitally connected Pacific should also be a secure and collaboratively governed one , built on shared standards rather than each nation solving the same problems alone . <p> The architecture exists . <p> What is now urgently needed is to deploy it . The Great Divergence that AI risks creating is not a distant concept . In March this year , Tonga hosted the Pacific launch of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of AI and digital technology is widening the gap between economies that build these systems and economies and societies that will simply live with the consequences . <p> Yet AI is already delivering results across the Pacific , from satellite and AI-enabled fisheries monitoring in Kiribati , which has helped recover around US$2 million annually in illegal fishing fines , to AI-assisted health diagnostics in countries including Nauru and Vanuatu . Digital innovation is advancing in Palau too , alongside partnerships with the European Union and UNCDF that are expanding digital services and AI-enabled financial inclusion across the region . <p> These are real wins , but they are wins within a system the Pacific did not design . The region already knows what it means to be on the wrong side of a divergence , in climate , in trade , in debt vulnerability . AI follows the same logic at greater speed . The difference is that the rules of AI are still being written , which means the divergence is not yet locked in . That window will not stay open for long . The time for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ once the frameworks are agreed and the tools are already being deployed . <p> The Pacific has spent decades demonstrating what it means to decide collectively , account for future generations , and treat traditional knowledge as sacred rather than extractive . These are not liabilities in the age of AI . <p> This is where a Pacific contribution can be pitched , while also driving for fairness in AI access and a gain in capabilities to even the odds in the AI race . Pacific representation in the forums shaping AI 's future can and must emphasise the distinction this region brings to shape AI models and applications of the future . <p> Kanni Wignaraja is UN Assistant Secretary-General and UNDP Regional Director for Asia and the Pacific and Munkhtuya Altangerel is the Resident Representative of UNDP 's Pacific Office in Fiji .

Photo / Getty Images <p> On a cold wet night in August 1979 , a large part of a Dunedin suburb rafted down a hillside on a layer of saturated clay . The slip at Abbotsford had been moving slowly since early June . Residents soon realised the buckling of the land below their houses was notgoing to stop and for weeks asked government and local officials to declare the suburb a disaster area . Finally , on the morning of Monday , August 6 , Green Island Borough Council mayor Vic Crimp announced a state of civil emergency . <p> Locals living on the slide were told not to panic but to prepare to evacuate . Some were evacuating when , at 9.07 pm on August 8 , after weeks of rain and snow , the hillside finally gave way , spectacularly . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the movement was likely to continue at a slow pace . But over a terrifying 15 minutes , homes were crushed and ripped apart as the slide accelerated to more than a metre a minute . <p> Nobody was killed or injured , but the trauma was great . Seventeen residents were stranded on the sliding block of land , listening to homes being torn apart , and 69 houses were ultimately lost . Hundreds of people were displaced . <p> When it finally ceased , about 18ha of land , half of it covered in houses , had slid 48m down the hill towards the Southern Motorway . A Civil Defence report found the landslide contained about 5 million cubic metres of sand and loose sediment . A 150m-wide , 16m-high scarp was left at the head of the slip . <p> Christchurch journalist Alan Wood was 16 and living in Green Island , across the valley , at the time . " I do n't know if we had heard it , or it was on the news , but it prompted me to go down there in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " <p> He walked down the hill , past the Harraway &amp; Sons oat mill and through the motorway underpass , on to the Abbotsford side of the valley . " I had the impression of a slip face above me . What I remember clearly is the electricity poles were all wonky and one wire had broken off which was bouncing around on the ground and arcing . That freaked me out . I called it quits . " <p> Much of the evidence of that night has since been absorbed into the landscape . However , Earth Sciences New Zealand ( ESNZ ) chief scientist , mountains to sea , Simon Cox , knows where to look . Residential streets running across the face of the hill end suddenly , where the landslide ripped away the ground . Old concrete walls are cracked and leaning downhill . Lumpy lobes of slumping land across the valley reveal how unstable things are here . <p> Cox says the aftermath of Abbotsford became highly political , as a Commission of Inquiry focused on assigning responsibility for an event that rocked Kiwi @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ old saying that a house was as safe as houses , " he says . " For many families , their home was their biggest investment , and suddenly they were watching houses slowly crack apart and become uninhabitable . It was n't a dramatic earthquake that destroyed them in 30 seconds , it happened over weeks and months , which made it all the more unsettling . " <h> Discover more <p> Eminent Kiwi engineering geologist Graham Hancox subsequently attributed the landslide to several factors , including a very weak and wet clay layer on which the surface rocks slid , a loss of stability caused by sand excavation from a quarry at the bottom of the hill from 1964-69 , and a leaking city council water main above the slide . <p> But as early as 1951 , a report based on a University of Otago geological survey had warned the Abbotsford soil was unsuitable for building on because of its instability . Yet residential subdivisions were allowed to go ahead . Cracks in housing were reported from 1968 onwards . <p> She 'll be right : The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ been allowed despite warnings the land was unstable . Photo / Supplied <h> Wet and slippery <p> Large parts of the country are at risk from regional landslide events where thousands of landslides occur over a short time , causing fatalities and damaging infrastructure and properties . <p> In February , University of Canterbury senior lecturer in disaster risk and resilience Tom Robinson emphasised how much of a threat to life landslides are , in an interview published in The Conversation . <p> " While it might be assumed that earthquakes or volcanic eruptions are Aotearoa 's deadliest natural hazards , landslides have claimed more than twice as many lives -- approximately 1800 -- as both combined over the past 200 years . " <p> The 2023 Auckland Anniversary Weekend and Cyclone Gabrielle deluges brought five deaths from slips and landslides , including two volunteer firefighters in Muriwai . In Auckland alone , more than 200 homes were subsequently declared uninhabitable due to slip damage or risk and another 1100 properties were yellow-stickered , with access restricted until remediated . <p> GNS Science reported that Cyclone Gabrielle caused 140,000 landslides @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ bill from slips and flooding at $9-14.5 billion . <p> The issue was brought home again in January this year , when six holidaymakers were killed as a large slip descended on the Mt Maunganui campground . Two more people -- a grandmother and her grandson -- died when another slip destroyed a house in nearby Welcome Bay . The storm went on to cause massive slips on the East Coast , cutting off communities . Further intense rains this month caused hundreds more landslides along the east coasts of both the South and North islands . <p> Although extreme rainfall is a common thread , in the case of Mauao , scientists are investigating whether underlying geology played a part . In nearby Tauranga , much of the landscape contains a clay mineral called halloysite , formed from weathered volcanic ash . With sufficient rainfall , it can turn to a greasy liquid . These halloysite layers are common in the central and northern North Island . <p> Other rock types make slopes landslide-prone -- for instance , mudstones around Gisborne and Whanganui . <p> NZ has this wicked @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and lots of people living on slopes . <p> British earth scientist Professor Dave Petley <h> Rising death toll <p> As climate change increases the threat to housing , productive land and infrastructure -- and the repair bills -- from slips , the question becomes what can we do as a society to lessen risk ? <p> The Natural Hazards Commission Toka Tu Ake says it now receives more damage claims for land movement than for any other hazard . The 13,000 claims for landslide damage in the five years to May was 10,000 more than in the previous five-year period . Land Information NZ says 25,600 properties have " natural hazard " including landslip or flooding risk noted on their titles , and , as the Herald reported last week , insurers are baulking at coverage for related claims on such properties . <p> The country 's vulnerability comes as no surprise to British earth scientist Dave Petley , a keynote speaker at a week-long international landslide conference in Queenstown in late April . Professor Petley , vice-chancellor of Nottingham Trent University and a prolific scientific blogger on landslides @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ these events around the world , the number of deaths is still increasing , with about 5100 fatalities last year . <p> The most likely cause was " the number of really intense , localised rainfall events triggering hundreds or thousands of landslides . And , of course , in New Zealand you 've seen that , too . New Zealand has the wicked combination for landslide losses of a highly dynamic topography , driven by the plate tectonic setting , and abundance of trigger events , such as extreme rainfall and earthquakes ; a degraded environment , especially the effects of deforestation ; and a vulnerable population -- lots of people living on slopes , and housing with a low resistance to landslides . <p> " I think landslides have also been heavily neglected as a hazard until recently , so mitigation and management are well behind . Unfortunately , these heavy rainfall events are going to continue to increase in frequency and severity , so I welcome the increased focus here . " <p> The Landslide Risk and Geo-Education Conference made New Zealand the focal point for world @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ geotechnical engineers and social scientists discussing the latest research on landslides and potential solutions , slope-stability modelling and land-use planning approaches to reduce risk for individuals and communities . <h> Playing catch-up <p> Conference convenor Ross Roberts , Auckland Council 's chief engineer , emphasises the importance of " clear , focused communication " of landslide risk . " We 've started from a little way behind other countries . That 's not surprising , because you 've got places like Italy , which is in many ways quite like New Zealand , but they 've been managing landslide risks for 2000 years . <p> " They 've got a bit more history behind it , more experience to make it work , and they 've also got dense populations , so they 've got more reason to get into it . But we 're catching up reasonably quickly , I think , and in some respects we 're well ahead of quite a lot of countries . " <p> " They 're going to get us up to a national level that 's probably equivalent to one of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ quite there yet is with our legal framework and our policy settings . That takes a bit of time , because you 've got to have the science underpinning it to make rational decisions . " The government 's replacement of the Resource Management Act with two new laws now before Parliament has raised some uncertainty , " so it 's hard to be consistent with how we approach it " . <p> The big challenge over the next decade or so is to get nationwide , consistent policy about mitigating landslide risk , rather than the current ad hoc practices adopted by councils , he says . " That makes it hard for the people setting the policies to be consistent , but it also means it 's really hard for the homeowners and others who live in the area to understand why things are being done the way they are in their region . When you have inconsistencies like that , people will question what you 're doing , which is absolutely fair . If one area starts putting in place land-use controls and another does n't , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ saves their life , so there 's going to be a natural pushback . " <p> ESNZ chief scientist , landslide hazards , Chris Massey , says people are becoming more alert to the dangers of landslides and to messaging about what to look for when land is on the move . However , understanding of the risks is not yet where it should be , says Massey , one of the principal investigators for the Sliding Lands and Landslide Watch projects funded by the Ministry of Business , Innovation and Employment 's Endeavour research fund . " We have been the poor cousin to other hazards , like earthquakes , volcanoes and tsunami over the last 20 to 30 years . We 're playing catch up . " <p> Although rapidly moving landslides can be dramatic , the larger , slowly creeping , deep-seated events also cause major damage . He points to Abbotsford and the 2011 rupture of the Maui gas pipeline in North Taranaki , with its huge economic effects on North Island businesses . <p> " Because landslides happen so often , people are becoming more @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ -- those signs of doors sticking and that kind of thing -- we 've built that knowledge over many , many decades from here and overseas . " <p> Both projects involve social and physical scientists : " We were not going to say , ' Here 's the science ' , and throw it to the social scientists ... We 've embedded the social scientists and aim to turn the physical science into something useful . " <p> The 13,000 claims in the five years to May was 10,000 more than in the previous five-year period . <h> Design for risk <p> Soft rocks , soils , slope angle and heavy rain combine to produce hundreds of thousands of landslides . Massey says Northland , Auckland and parts of the central and lower North Island are especially vulnerable to rainfall-induced slides . For large , drier parts of the South Island , landslides from earthquakes become more of a hazard . <p> Apart from the increasing frequency of extreme weather triggering landslides , there are also issues with rising population and people building in new areas , he says @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not so good for homes . <p> " It 's not about saying , ' You ca n't live here . ' It 's about saying , ' If you want to live here , you 're going to have to take into account there might be a problem ' . <p> " If you 're not on a slope , you 're probably all right . But if you 're close to the bottom of the slope , you might want to be a little more worried . And if you 're living on an actual slope , you might want to think about where you are on that slope . <p> " Talk to your councils . Councils now have quite a lot of information -- some have more than others . It 's online . Just be aware of your environment . " <p> What 's the difference between hazard and risk ? Hazard is the source of anything which can potentially cause harm , while risk is the probability of that harm happening multiplied by the consequences , such as death and injury , damage to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ professor of disaster risk and resilience Tim Davies . <p> He believes the drive by experts to make probability calculations of risk has confused the public by overcomplicating matters . <p> " Everybody is spending lots of time and money and intellectual effort trying to generate frequency data for things like landslides , when it does n't do any good . Frequency only applies reliably to a large number of events , but we are really only interested in a single event -- the next one . <p> " People in Franz Josef are n't concerned about what 's going to happen in the Alpine Fault earthquake after the next one , because the next event is all people are interested in . <p> " If you throw probability out of the window , then the community can be fully engaged in the whole process . And the one body that has probably only intuitive knowledge about the way it functions is the community . They 've got to be involved all the way through . " <p> " If I 've got young children or an elderly parent , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ issues , my risk tolerance might be lower , so I 'm going to evacuate earlier . Whereas , with someone else , it could be , ' I do n't really care ; I 'll be fine . Even if I 'm stuck here for the next two weeks , I 'm fine . ' " <p> Potter is director of Canary Innovation , which specialises in evaluating forecasts and warnings for natural hazards and developing them for effective communication . <p> " The issue here is that most landslides usually might affect only one or two people , one house , for example . It 's less likely to make national news or stick in the media for a long time . <p> " But an event like the Mauao landslide , where it unfortunately kills more people , becomes more attention-grabbing . And people start to think , ' Oh , that could happen to me , and these poor people that were in the camping ground , it could have been any of us just on holiday ' . <p> " Landslides are so point-specific , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ really hard to manage and , from a public perspective , you really need more options than relying on those engineering-type mitigation measures that can be very expensive . " <p> Rural ruin : Steve Dill on his family 's Kaipara sheep and beef farm , which was riddled with slips after the 2023 Auckland deluges and Cyclone Gabrielle . Photo / Getty Images <h> Putting it out there <p> Could there ever be a reliable warning system for landslides , like those for severe weather , or is the problem too insidious ? <p> Potter says that is being studied by ESNZ , to calculate in some detail the probability of a landslide occurring within a 32km x 32km grid cell , depending on the amount of rain falling or earthquake shaking occurring . <p> " It depends on the rainfall and the geology and the slope angle , vegetation , local drainage and several other factors . But the highest probability , in one example forecast for Cyclone Gabrielle , was 17% . So , if you knew there was a 17% chance of a slope failing on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ by a river or a bridge , might be by your house -- would you act on that ? " <p> Potter and ESNZ hazard and risk management scientist Sara Harrison have been interviewing people about what they would do with such information . " Emergency managers might say they 're not going to evacuate someone on that , at least until the models are more robust . But I feel if that was my house and there was a 17% chance of a landslide occurring and hitting it , I 'd want to know that . I 'd want to get out of there . <p> " That 's why I 'm so keen on this kind of information being supported in an operational sense , so it can get to the public and they can make their own decisions , not having somebody making that decision for them further up the chain . " <p> Councils need to be the first port of call for landslide hazard information but that 's not always ideal , she says . <p> " Every council 's got a different hazard portal @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ about it in your location , but what if you 're on holiday elsewhere ? You do n't know how that council might get its information . A national portal for that kind of thing would 100% be helpful , and not just for landslides but having all of our hazard information on there . " <p> Asked about a nationwide strategy for landslides , Climate Change Minister Simon Watts said the National Policy Statement for Natural Hazards was helping to usher in a more co-ordinated response . <p> " It requires councils to make sure new development minimises risks from natural hazards , including landslides , to help us minimise future costs and boost community resilience . <p> " Right now , our immediate priority is flooding . We are seeing more frequent and severe events . This is why the government is developing the New Zealand Flood Map . " <p> Emergency Management and Recovery Minister Mark Mitchell says responsibility for identifying , assessing and managing local hazards and risks , including landslides , lies with councils . <p> Reinforcing this approach is the new Emergency Management Bill @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ devastating impact , " says Mitchell . " At a national level , the National Emergency Management Agency supports local government to undertake their responsibilities on a day-to-day basis , with government providing additional support to local and regional emergency responses if , and as , required . " <h> Defining case study <p> ESNZ 's Cox says the Abbotsford landslide changed the way the country thinks about natural disasters . <p> " The landslide did n't fit neatly into the way natural disasters were thought about at the time . Insurance and government assistance had largely evolved around sudden catastrophic events : earthquakes , storms and floods . Abbotsford was different . It was a slowly developing natural hazard , and there were difficult questions about when the disaster actually began , who should carry the cost and where responsibility lay . Those questions became highly political . They contributed to a much wider discussion about natural-hazard insurance , government responsibilities and land-use planning . Abbotsford was n't the sole reason those systems changed , but it became one of the defining case studies that exposed their limitations . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Abbotsford feel remarkably modern . We still struggle with hazards that develop gradually rather than catastrophically -- whether that 's coastal erosion , sea-level rise or climate change . The science may tell us the hazard is real long before the crisis point arrives , but deciding when to act , who pays and how society responds remains just as difficult today . " <p> Auckland Council 's Roberts says he will continue to advocate strongly for a national landslide strategy , inspired by those in the United States and Canada . New Zealand currently has a Landslides National Advisory Group which is helping drive efforts towards that . <p> " The trouble is , we 've got all these silos who own parts of the risk . You 've got your lifeline utilities , your land-use planners , your emergency responders , civil defence groups . You 've got councils with different responsibilities . You 've got central government , the Department of Conservation , the Natural Hazards Commission -- there 's more than I could possibly list . They 've each got part of the picture , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , but it does n't mean it 's solving the problem . <p> " Sometimes we contradict each other . Sometimes it 's an assumption that someone else is doing something when they 're not . So , there are big gaps . Having a national strategy that brings it all together and says , ' These are each of our roles , this is how it fits into a coherent plan , ' would make a huge difference . "

@@139129354 <p> Sometimes when we are in a new city as we rush from one site to another we forget to look up . John Walsh 's walking guides encourage us to do just that as well as provide some interesting snippets on the buildings we pass . <p> In Te Whanganui-a-Tara Wellington Architecture : A walking guide , John Walsh looks at city 's newest buildings and some of its oldest , as the city continues to be challenged by seismic risk and winds . <h> St James Theatre <p> Energetic , restless Henry Eli White ( 1877-1952 ) could have sprung from the pages of a Jazz Age novel like The Great Gatsby or , more likely , a Damon Runyon short story . Big Harry was six feet tall and sixteen stone ( 101kg ) , with ambition to match . He started out working for his father , a Dunedin builder , while taking evening classes in architecture and engineering . After setting up his own contracting company , he moved to Christchurch around 1900 . There he designed and constructed commercial buildings before specialising in theatre @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ First World War . He quickly graduated from creating theatres and cinemas around New Zealand to winning commissions in Australia . By 1914 , his home and main office was in Sydney . He was prolific , although less than he boasted and lived large in a harbourside house , with a Rolls-Royce and yacht , and enjoyed big game fishing as well as overseas travel . <p> Just as he was completing the State Theatre ( 1929 ) , an extravagant and still extant picture palace on Sydney 's Market St , Wall Street crashed . The Great Depression killed his practice , but he tried other things , including farming in the Waikato and dolomite mining in New South Wales . His life tapered to its end in a small flat in Sydney 's Kings Cross . <p> Wellington 's St James Theatre , originally His Majesty 's , was an important milestone in White 's career . It was constructed of steel frame and reinforced concrete in only nine months in 1912 . The restrained Edwardian Baroque exterior only hints at the richness of a Rococo interior @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ theatre-planning ability is evidenced by the shallow arc of the seating which imparts intimacy and the provision of unimpeded sight lines . <p> In the 1980s a determined heritage campaign saved St James from demolition by a notorious developer , the Chase Corporation . The 2300-seat theatre , which is entered from the foyer of the neighbouring 1980s building , has recently been restored and seismically strengthened . <p> The sheen of Takina 's bronze glazing . Photo : Patrick Reynolds <h> Takina <p> 50 Cable St and Wakefield St Studio Pacific Architecture , 2023 <p> Like any city , Wellington is composed of built quadrilaterals . Economy and efficiency dictate a default to the rectangular box . It does n't have to be this way , as evidenced by Basil Spence 's circular Beehive and Jorn Utzon 's Sydney Opera House ( 1973 ) , a spherical solution that pushed design boundaries to their analog limits . Latterly , the practice founded by Iraqi-born British architect Zaha Hadid ( 1950-2016 ) has flamboyantly realised the geometric potential of digital technology , pushing , pulling and kneading buildings into sinuous @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in countries where design ambition is complemented by autocratic vainglory , the wow-factor appeal of this style cuts across national borders and political divides . <p> Takina stretches languorously between apartment buildings on busy Cable St , opposite altogether less singular Te Papa . ( It also faces a dispiriting stretch of Wakefield St. ) The sheen of Takina 's bronze glazing , especially on its north-facing Cable St side , is reminiscent of the facade of the InterContinental Hotel , although relating the two buildings is like comparing the CGI of the Avatar movies to the marionette puppetry of the 1960s TV series Thunderbirds . <p> Inside , six-level Takina is a series of functional conference and exhibition spaces . The building was intended to include a film museum featuring film-maker Peter Jackson 's collection of cinema memorabilia . When Jackson withdrew from the project , the trajectory of city council-owned Takina was tweaked towards the nebulous realm of venue architecture , a journey always undertaken as much in hope as expectation : build it and fingers crossed , they will come . <h> BNZ Place <p> 1 Whitmore @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ building constructed in 2006 on reclaimed land near the old Pipitea wharf was demolished after damage caused by the 2016 Kaikoura earthquake proved irreparable ( earlier damage occurred in the 2013 Seddon earthquake ) . The building had been designed by Jasmax and was named for and substantially occupied by the Bank of New Zealand ( BNZ ) . Evidently there were no hard feelings , for in 2024 the bank moved into and bought naming rights to , a new building two blocks away , also designed by Jasmax ( the city council and the engineers of the 2006 building are engaged in a liability dispute which promises to be lengthily lucrative for a couple of large law firms ) . <p> As would be expected , the design of the new 13-storey building BNZ Place was informed by much modelling of seismic hazards and wind effects . Climate change was also considered . BNZ Place is base-isolated on piles , driven 60m down into bedrock , which connect to a steel diagrid that can be viewed from the building 's ground-level public cafe . Negating , or at @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the building curvaceously , even sensuously , so that the wind might slip around it , rather than smash against it . <p> As for climate change the building is raised on a plinth as a precaution against sea-level rise . The building occupies almost all the triangular block bounded by Whitmore , Featherston and Ballance Sts , and is surrounded at street-level by a steel-supported , glass-topped canopy . Critic Guy Marriage likened this appendage to the sculptural structures of Santiago Calatrava . Marriage also discerned in BNZ Place a thwarted vertical ambition ; like 20 Customhouse Quay , the building deserves to be taller . BNZ Place 's blue glazing makes for a very shiny and reflective surface . Someone should alert Malcolm Walker , New Zealand 's premier architectural cartoonist , to the return of one of his favourite characters from the 1980s the Marquis de Facade . 
@@139129400 <h> Do we still love our country enough to fight and die for it ? <p> Then out spake brave Horatius , The Captain of the Gate : " To every man upon this earth Death cometh soon or late . And how can man die better Than facing fearful odds , For the ashes of his fathers , And the temples of his Gods , " <p> From Horatius by Thomas Babington Macaulay , 1842 . <p> There was a time when schoolboys across the English-speaking world were regaled with Horatius 's heroic pledge to patria -- the fatherland . Not recently , of course . Macaulay 's stirring evocation of Roman virtues ( standing-in for the virtues of the then rapidly expanding British Empire ) would today be dismissed as a hymn to patriarchy , hierarchy , imperialism , suicidal ideation , and war . Certainly not ideas to be promoted before a classroom of sensitive twenty-first century students . <p> Therein lies the fundamental problem confronting every western government anxious ( and under fierce American pressure ) to boost their defences . New Zealand 's own Defence @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ latest Nato summit , bringing with him the many warnings and frustrations of his northern counterparts . <p> Voting more money to one 's country 's defence forces may be a good start , but it is not enough . All the weapons in the world are useless if there is no one to wield them . Where is the West going to find the young men and women needed to defend it ? <p> It was much easier in the past when , if your country called , you answered . Even when the call to arms came in the form of a conscription notice the level of compliance was , by today 's standards , astonishingly high . <p> In the early years of the twentieth century Germany fielded Europe 's most effective army by far . It was led by the aristocratic officers of Prussia , but it was bulked-up by millions of young middle- and working-class conscripts . Compulsory military training had become a rite of passage for all young German men : a patriotic duty that most of them accepted without protest . <p> One @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Merz , envies his Imperial German predecessors . Determined to put Germany at the head of a credible military combination of Nato allies -- sans the USA -- he has been confronted with the dispiriting advice that , the nation 's impressive martial history notwithstanding , the level of resistance likely to greet any attempt to resurrect conscription makes the whole idea a non-starter . Young Germans , raised to view their country 's past as a terrible warning against militarism and war have become pacifists -- almost by default . <p> And New Zealand 's young people ? Would they , too , default to pacifism ? If Chris Penk , or a future Defence Minister , responding to a rapidly deteriorating geopolitical environment , decided to reintroduce compulsory military training ( as Labour Prime Minister Peter Fraser did at the onset of the Cold War in the late-1940s ) how many young Kiwi men and women would happily submit themselves to two years in uniform ? Two years of being yelled at while learning to kill their fellow human-beings ? <p> Penk and his party would doubtless reject @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to their commitment to rebuilding New Zealand 's volunteer defence force after years of declining capacity and shrinking budgets . <p> " Not before time ! " , would be the response of those who take an interest in matters military . <p> Today 's NZ Defence Force is but a shadow of its former highly-effective and highly-respected self . Its loss of effectiveness was illustrated for all the world to see by the foundering of the HMNZS Manawanui -- probably the most embarrassing display of naval incompetence in New Zealand history . <p> And it 's not just the Navy that has problems . Our ground forces are currently at less than half their recommended strength . Even the point of the NZDF spear , New Zealand 's internationally renowned Special Air Service Regiment , has been plagued with recruitment and retention difficulties , degrading its efficiency . <p> Only the Royal New Zealand Air Force has reason to feel confident with its kit -- even if it was forced to wait for decades to get it . Like all the other service arms , the RNZAF struggles to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ needed to maintain and use it . <p> The Coalition Government is hoping that monies already appropriated for improving the wages and conditions of NZDF personnel -- serving and civilian -- will take the sting out of the military 's recruitment and retention problems . Chances are that it wo n't . <p> There is growing concern within the NZDF that the country 's most senior military personnel , the men and women responsible for orchestrating New Zealand 's defence capabilities , have failed to understand what has happened to their own services , and what is happening across the wider world . <p> The failure of the top brass to impress upon the Ardern-led government the extreme risk of ordering soldiers to guard quarantine facilities , and then to fully appreciate the catastrophic loss of morale and commitment which that failure had caused , has convinced a strategically significant number of service personnel to abandon their careers for institutions overseen by more serious people . <p> If a small nation of just five million is to maintain an effective military then it must either declare itself wholly and unabashedly @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ enthusiasm their strategic and tactical military doctrines ; or it must take its commitment to an " independent " foreign and defence policy framework seriously and initiate a radical reassessment and reorganisation of New Zealand 's diplomatic and military posture . <p> The option the present Coalition Government appears to have chosen mirrors that of just about all of the governments preceding it : to go all-in with our traditional friends and allies . What they have not chosen to do , however , is make it clear to the New Zealand public that this is the course they have decided to follow . Indeed , Prime Minister Luxon continues to insist that his own and his government 's commitment to " New Zealand 's independent foreign policy " remains undiminished . <p> There will be many who argue that going all-in with our friends and allies is the only viable option for New Zealand . Who else , apart from Australia and the United States , would New Zealanders be comfortable fighting alongside ? <p> That remains a fair question , and until 2025 the answer was always obvious @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and the moral trajectory of his lawless administration becoming ever more concerning , attaching New Zealand to the United States has ceased to be the straightforward strategic option of decades past . <p> WHY DO HUMAN-BEINGS FIGHT ? They fight out of love , or from fear . <p> In the entrance-hall of the first institution erected to look after the New Zealand veterans of a foreign war -- in this case the Anglo-Boer War of 1899-1902 -- stands a carved wooden memorial to those who fell . Inscribed upon its base are the words : " They died for the Empire . " <p> The New Zealanders who fought in South Africa did not do so out of fear for the Boers , but for the love they bore their King-Emperor and the British Empire over which he reigned . <p> In the wars that followed , more and more fear became mixed-in with the love . <p> Without that fear : without the hatred it aroused against Imperial Germany , the Nazis , Imperial Japan , the Soviet Union and " Godless Communism " ; the vast @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ wars -- hot and cold -- could not have taken place . <p> How do you conscript your best and your brightest in the absence of fear and hate ? How much love of country is required to rally the flower of a nation 's youth to their country 's flag ? <p> The Ukrainians know the quantum required of both emotions to make a people stand and fight . Horatius , the Captain of the Gate to Rome , knew what he was prepared to sacrifice for " the ashes of his fathers , and the temples of his gods " . Maori New Zealanders also know . <p> But the rest of us ? Do we fear Xi 's China enough ; do we love Trump 's America enough ; to fight against the one and for the other ? <p> Do enough of us still love New Zealand enough to fight and die for it ? <p> *Chris Trotter has been writing and commenting professionally about New Zealand politics for more than 30 years . @@139129438 <h> Finance Minister Nicola Willis had little option but to protect local cement supply <p> Caption:The best Finance Minister Nicola Willis could do was ensure there were plenty of safeguards so taxpayers ' money is as well spent as possible , writes Business Editor Corin Dann.Photo credit:RNZ / Samuel Rillstone <p> Analysis - It 's hard to imagine Finance Minister Nicola Willis was particularly thrilled about handing over $60 million to one of New Zealand 's major listed companies , Fletcher Building , so its subsidiary Golden Bay Cement could keep manufacturing cement at its plant near Whangarei . <p> But in the end , a subsidy of some kind looks like a practical decision she probably had little choice but to make . <p> Golden Bay Cement is New Zealand 's only cement manufacturing facility , supplying roughly 60 percent of the cement used here . <p> Closing the plant and moving to an import-only facility from 2030 , as Fletcher Building had signalled might need to happen if it did not get help , would surely not be in the interests of the country . <p> New Zealand @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ - would we really want to rely only on imported cement , in a world where supply chains are disrupted and protectionism is on the rise ? <p> The best Willis could do was ensure there were plenty of safeguards around this deal , so taxpayers ' money is as well spent as possible . <p> And there are some , it seems . <p> Fletcher Building has committed to investing at least $150m of its own money in the plant through to at least 2040 , covering operations , decarbonisation and resilience , with claw-back provisions if it does not deliver . <p> The decarbonisation part is important , as the cost of being part of the Emissions Trading Scheme is a key factor in Golden Bay Cement 's struggles and inability to compete with foreign competitors . <p> Cement imports do not face the same ETS charges . <p> Willis says she did consider changing ETS settings to help Golden Bay Cement , but in the end decided doing so might undermine the wider integrity of the scheme and cost the government more . <p> A specific one-off @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Fletcher Building <p> The decision to keep the cement flowing in Northland is not yet drawing much criticism , with even ACT , the party of the free market , saying it supports keeping New Zealand 's only cement manufacturer operating . <p> A ' ' necessary evil " was how ACT leader David Seymour described it . <p> ACT 's issue , however , is with the ETS and its impact on Golden Bay Cement 's ability to compete . <p> It claims this is evidence that New Zealand 's climate change settings are not working for the country because they are loading emissions costs onto businesses . <p> The Employers and Manufacturers Association is strongly welcoming the move . <p> It sees it as a practical step . One that is also an important signal that New Zealand is more focused on resilience and looking after sectors critical to its economic future . <p> Perhaps that is not surprising , as just this month it issued an election policy document encouraging the government to offer financial incentives to firms critical to the economy . <p> The idea was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The government has been at pains to stress this is a one-off case . <p> Some in the forestry and mill industry , of course , may feel a little aggrieved . They too have faced multiple closures in recent years due to rising costs . <p> However , the government would likely argue that wood simply does n't have the same supply chain weakness as cement , where New Zealand has just one manufacturer . <p> All of this does suggest something has shifted a little in New Zealand 's political and economic landscape when it comes to government involvement in backing core industries . <p> More broadly , there seems to be a deeper recognition that New Zealand must now be smarter about its self-reliance in a world of oil shocks and Trump tariffs . <p> Industries such as steel , cement or fertiliser suddenly look a lot more important . <p> The real challenge here , of course , is making sure the right sectors and industries get help , and that the government does not simply hand out money to companies that should be lifting their @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ applied strongly , and the companies involved must do all they can to modernise , electrify and compete on their own in future .

After years of decline in mussel populations , a sustained restoration effort has seen shellfish numbers recover dramatically , offering renewed hope for the harbour 's ecological and cultural future . <p> Customary Fisheries Officer Charles Bluett says maintaining the current harvesting restrictions is critical to ensuring the recovering mussel beds continue to strengthen before any consideration is given to reopening customary or recreational harvesting . <p> The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Ngati Awa , scientists , government agencies and local communities . Restoration efforts have focused on rebuilding mussel populations while protecting the wider harbour ecosystem that supports a diverse range of marine life . <p> Central to the project has been the application of kaitiakitanga , with Maori environmental knowledge guiding long-term decisions about how the harbour should be managed and restored . The approach has balanced matauranga Maori alongside scientific monitoring to measure ecological recovery and adapt management practices over time . <p> The project is increasingly being recognised as an example of how Indigenous knowledge and Western science can work together to restore degraded ecosystems . The lessons learned in Ohiwa Harbour are expected to inform similar restoration initiatives elsewhere in Aotearoa , particularly in areas where shellfish populations have suffered significant declines . <p> The iwi says protecting the gains already achieved will require patience and continued stewardship , ensuring the harbour remains resilient in the face of environmental pressures including climate change , sedimentation and increasing human activity . <p> As discussions continue around extending the existing protections , Ngati Awa hopes Ohiwa Harbour will stand @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ communities , iwi and scientists work together to restore taonga species and protect the health of the moana.

@@139189469 <p> A composite image of a herd of cattle and a car in Whangarei Heads overlayed with dollar signs . Composite image source : Unsplash , 123rf.com and interest.co.nz <p> Climate disruption and economic impacts in New Zealand are growing , a report from the Climate Change Commission says , and greater and more durable emissions reductions are needed to manage these risks . <p> " Delaying action increases disruption and costs for households and the economy , and narrows the country 's options , " the commission said in its 2026 monitoring report looking at emissions reduction released on Wednesday . This report tracks how emissions reductions are going and the Government 's progress towards meeting NZ 's second and third emissions budgets , and the 2050 budget . <p> Emissions budgets are the total amount of emissions that 's allowed to be released during the budget 's period , according to the Ministry for Cities , Environment , Regions and Transport ( MCERT ) . <p> The report found that risks to the country 's climate goals for the next decade have increased , and the second and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 2030 biogenic methane target unlikely to be met . Alongside this , the report said the pace of emissions reductions needed to more than double over the next few years . <p> The report highlights current plans and policies are " insufficient to meet the third emissions budget ( 2031-2035 ) or the 2050 target , and the window for action is closing . " <p> " The gap in the emissions reduction plan for the third budget has not been significantly addressed since our last report and the risks to planned reductions have increased . " <p> " If additional action does not occur in the next one to two years , it will no longer be feasible to meet the 2030 biogenic methane target or the third emissions budget . " <p> The report said policy actions that could reduce emissions included things like improved energy regulatory measures , extending the road user charges exemption for electric heavy vehicles , passing legislation to use time-of-use charging for transport systems and the Gas Transition Loan Guarantee Scheme ( this was announced after the commission 's assessment period ) . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ emissions . Resilience . <p> Unlike many overseas jurisdictions , New Zealand did not introduce emergency subsidies . Businesses adapted . Some already had fuel hedging in place . Others absorbed the cost . <p> Seasonal timing has also helped . Winter @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ calendar , providing breathing space while markets stabilise . <p> But Smith warns that elevated energy costs are now embedded in the economics of farming . <p> The greatest pressure falls not on exporters enjoying buoyant international prices , but on producers selling into the domestic economy - vegetable growers , arable farmers and inshore fishing operators . <p> These are domestically exposed businesses whose customers are the five million New Zealanders already grappling with higher living costs . " They 're the industries I worry about , " Smith says . " They ca n't simply pass higher costs through . @@139208505 <p> New Zealand is at growing risk of missing almost every major climate target , with a new Climate Change Commission monitoring report warning that current government policies are no longer sufficient to meet future emissions budgets or the country 's long-term 2050 climate commitments . The report paints a concerning picture , concluding that while greenhouse gas emissions have ... <p> New Zealand is at growing risk of missing almost every major climate target , with a new Climate Change Commission monitoring report warning that current government policies are no longer sufficient to meet future emissions budgets or the country 's long-term 2050 climate commitments . <p> The report paints a concerning picture , concluding that while greenhouse gas emissions have fallen in recent years , the pace of reduction is too slow and many of the gains have been driven by temporary factors rather than lasting structural change . It warns that the second emissions budget for 2026 to 2030 is now at increasing risk , while current policies are unlikely to deliver either the third emissions budget or the 2050 net-zero target without significant new action . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the emissions reductions the Government is relying on to meet the second emissions budget are now considered to be at significant risk . It also concluded that current projections show New Zealand is unlikely to achieve its 2030 biogenic methane reduction target , with expected reductions falling short of the statutory goal . <p> The findings have reignited criticism of the Luxon Government 's climate programme , with environmental organisations and opposition parties arguing the Government has weakened or reversed many of the policies needed to reduce emissions . <p> A report released alongside the political debate argues that changes made since the coalition took office -- including the removal or scaling back of climate initiatives across transport , energy and environmental policy -- have undermined New Zealand 's ability to meet both domestic emissions budgets and its international climate commitments . Supporters of the report say the cumulative effect of those policy reversals has increased climate risks while delaying investment in cleaner technologies and resilient infrastructure . <p> The Climate Change Commission also warned that delaying emissions reductions will ultimately increase costs for households , businesses and the wider @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ reduce exposure to volatile global energy markets and lower the long-term cost of transitioning to a low-emissions economy . The report notes that heavy reliance on forestry offsets , combined with uncertainty surrounding the New Zealand Emissions Trading Scheme , leaves the country vulnerable if expected emissions reductions fail to materialise . <p> Among the Commission 's key concerns is that recent reductions in industrial emissions have largely resulted from lower production rather than technological improvements or permanent emissions reductions . Gross emissions also stalled during 2024 following increased coal-fired electricity generation during a dry year , highlighting ongoing vulnerabilities in the country 's energy system . <p> The Government has maintained that it remains committed to meeting New Zealand 's climate obligations while pursuing a more practical and cost-effective approach . Climate Change Minister Simon Watts has recently defended legislative changes to the Climate Change Response Act , arguing they will streamline planning requirements , reduce duplication and improve the efficiency of New Zealand 's climate framework while requiring councils to better prepare for climate risks . <p> However , the Commission cautions that the window for meaningful action @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not introduced within the next one to two years , meeting future emissions budgets and several key climate targets may no longer be feasible . <p> For Maori communities , many of whom face disproportionate impacts from flooding , coastal erosion , drought and infrastructure vulnerability , the report reinforces concerns that delayed climate action could further widen existing inequities while increasing the cost of adaptation for whanau , hapu and iwi. 
@@139208527 <h> #opinion : I 've developed a chronic case of Electxiety <p> I 've developed a chronic case of Electxiety By Matengahere Mete In May 2026 , the New Zealand government changed the law . Not for future cases . For all cases . Including Mike Smith 's . Smith , a Maori elder of Ngapuhi and Ngati Kahu , spent years taking this country 's largest emitters -- Fonterra , Genesis Energy , Z Energy among them -- ... <h> I 've developed a chronic case of Electxiety <p> By Matengahere Mete <p> In May 2026 , the New Zealand government changed the law . Not for future cases . For all cases . Including Mike Smith 's . <p> Smith , a Maori elder of Ngapuhi and Ngati Kahu , spent years taking this country 's largest emitters -- Fonterra , Genesis Energy , Z Energy among them -- to court . He argued they had caused damage to his whenua , his coastline , his community . Rising seas . Flooding of cultural sites . The slow erosion of everything his whakapapa is tied to . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , setting a trial for April 2027 . But the government amended the Climate Change Response Act 2002 to make that case -- and all cases like it -- impossible . The government said the lawsuit was creating " uncertainty in business confidence . " But documents released after the announcement revealed that Fonterra and Z Energy , two of the defendants in Smith 's case , had met with the Prime Minister 's office . A briefing note explaining the rationale for the legislation had been provided to that office . Smith called it a " co-ordinated campaign of secret lobbying , political interference and corporate influence at the highest levels of power . " The Prime Minister says he has no record of the meetings . The trial that was coming will not now come . Smith has since taken the fight to the United Nations . Together with the National Iwi Chairs Forum , Climate Clinic Aotearoa and Lawyers for Climate Action , he has filed a formal complaint with the UN Special Rapporteur on Climate Change and Human Rights , asking her to intervene before the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> When the rules of the game can be rewritten overnight to protect powerful corporations , the act of participating in that game starts to feel heavy . It breeds a specific kind of exhaustion . <p> And so , I 've got election anxiety . Electxiety . The knowledge that you must act . The inability to see where . The friction of those two things grinding against each other . And the exhaustion of living inside that friction election after election . <p> But this election has made me do something I did n't used to do . I 've started talking to people -- anyone , really . Strangers . The woman at the checkout counter , flicking my butter , eggs and milk through the scanner . I stop her . Are you voting ? She looks at me , her eyes are engaged . Holding her stare , I ask : Who are you voting for ? She responds , " anyone but this government , I 'm over them . " <p> I do n't know exactly when it started . But I do @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ -- the queue , the counter , the moment before the eftpos machine beeps . At first I thought it was just my way of raising awareness ... but if I was to be honest I am looking for something . Confidence , maybe . Or company . Someone else who believes enough for both of us . <p> Because if I am honest about that too -- I am not sure I believe . Not fully . <p> I am caught inside a system that demands I engage , knowing it may not deliver . To vote feels like a kind of untruth -- a performance of faith I do not fully possess . But to not vote feels like failure . Like I gave up before I even woke up . Either way I do not feel true to myself . Hence , electxiety . <p> It is an epistemic shudder -- where knowledge produces both the paralysing uncertainty and the fierce compulsion to move forward . Knowing that I am betraying myself by voting into a system that forsakes Maori -- and yet knowing that I am @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of forsaking . Electxiety -- that friction in election season when what you know gets in the way of what you want to believe , yet you are still bound to act . Until there is a better way . <p> This is not the first time . It will not be the last . The history of Maori engagement with the New Zealand legal and political system is littered with moments like this one -- where the tool worked , where the argument held , where the system said yes , and then found another way to say no . The foreshore and seabed . The Treaty principles . Now climate litigation . <p> Both major parties have done this . In different registers , with different language , toward the same end . The system does not fail Maori by accident . It morphs and reconfigures . Akin to a virus . <p> The American writer and theorist bell hooks wrote about what it means to live and act from the margin -- the place where those pushed to the edges of a society develop a particular clarity @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is not only a place of deprivation . It is also a place of insight . Of seeing clearly what those at the centre can not or will not see . <p> I see clearly . That is the problem . And the gift . <p> hooks also wrote about the difference between withdrawing from a system and refusing to be consumed by it . You can participate without surrendering . You can vote without pretending the vote is enough . You can put the ballot in the box with your eyes open . <p> So I will vote in November . Not because the epistemic shudder has stopped . Not because I believe the system will hold , or that the goalposts will stay where they are , or that what happened to Mike Smith will not happen again in some other form to some other Maori person who found the right tool at the right moment . <p> I will vote because the alternative is silence . And silence , in a system that is already very good at not hearing us , is not neutrality . It @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> And maybe I will walk away with a wholeness I have reclaimed -- not because voting will bring closure , but because voting will put me in the eye of the storm . The electxiety will stop palpitating in my heart , the epistemic shudder in my head will quiet , the act will bring a still -- for the moment . <p> Somewhere in the mess of placing myself inside the political storm , I will force the necessary movement and from that I will determine direction into the struggle without end . Ka whawhai tonu matou . <h> References <p> Smith v Fonterra Co-operative Group Ltd 2024 NZSC 5 <p> Relates to : " The Supreme Court in 2024 ruled unanimously in his favour , setting a trial for April 2027.
