@@139251521 <h> The future of skiing depends on the choices we make today <p> Every winter , the same questions return . Will this be a good snow season ? When will the next storm arrive ? Will there be enough snow for the school holidays ? <p> Snowfall has always captured our attention . It fuels weather forecasts , dominates conversations around Queenstown and Wanaka and determines whether skiers and snowboarders rush to the mountains or postpone their plans . Yet while we often focus on the next snowfall , we rarely stop to ask a more important question : what role do our mountains play in the future of Aotearoa New Zealand , and what are we doing to ensure they continue to fulfil that role ? <p> The Southern Alps are far more than a winter playground . They are the source of New Zealand 's major rivers , underpin hydroelectric power generation and freshwater resources and are home to internationally significant mountain environments . They also support one of New Zealand 's most recognisable tourism industries . Images of snow-covered peaks and ski fields have become inseparable @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ region , attracting visitors from around the world and contributing substantially to regional economies . <p> But the future of skiing is increasingly being questioned as the climate warms . <p> Warmer winters will inevitably affect seasonal snow . Higher snowlines , more frequent rain-on-snow events and an increase in winter snowmelt will all place pressure on ski areas , particularly those at lower elevations . These are genuine challenges that deserve careful attention . <p> However , history shows that the ski industry has never stood still , evolving through several distinct phases of development . Organised recreational skiing began on Mt Ruapehu in 1913 with the formation of the country 's first ski club , while the winter recreation potential of the Mt Cook region was beginning to be recognised . The Mt Cook region experienced a ski boom during the 1930s , establishing Ball Glacier as one of New Zealand 's premier winter destinations . Coronet Peak opened in 1947 as New Zealand 's first commercial ski field , followed by decades in which ski clubs played a major role in expanding access to skiing and developing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Rock ski field on the Rock and Pillar Range . The development of Mt Hutt , Treble Cone and Cardrona during the 1970s , followed by The Remarkables in 1985 , expanded commercial skiing and established the South Island as the country 's principal destination for alpine recreation . <p> History also reminds us that not every ski field survives . Ball Glacier illustrates how the fortunes of ski areas can change over time . Its eventual decline reflected a combination of glacier retreat , the loss of Ball Lodge and repeated damage to the access road . The Rock and Pillar Range tells a different , but equally important , story of changing snow reliability and evolving recreational demand . The lesson is not that ski areas inevitably disappear , but that the long-term success of ski areas has always depended on their ability to adapt to changing environmental , technological and economic conditions . <p> Climate change undoubtedly raises the stakes , but it does not necessarily signal the end of skiing in New Zealand . <p> Research into seasonal snow suggests a more nuanced picture than is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to rise over coming decades , increasing the importance of elevation and terrain . Yet long-term observations show that changes are not always straightforward . At The Remarkables ski area , for example , a continuous snow record spanning more than four decades shows no long-term decline in seasonal snowfall . What remains uncertain is how much more winter precipitation is falling as rain rather than snow and how much faster the snowpack is melting between storms as temperatures rise . These processes may ultimately prove more important than snowfall totals in determining the quality and persistence of the seasonal snowpack . <p> This distinction matters because it changes how we think about adaptation . <p> Importantly , adaptation options already exist . Modern snowmaking technology is far more efficient than it was even two decades ago , allowing resorts to make better use of marginal weather windows . Equally important is where future ski infrastructure is developed . While higher elevations will become increasingly important , not all terrain is equally resilient . Aspect , shading and wind redistribution strongly influence where snow accumulates and how long it persists @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that is naturally more resilient , guiding smarter investment and long-term planning for New Zealand 's ski industry . <p> This is not simply about preserving a winter sport . It is about sustaining a major tourism industry , supporting regional economies and ensuring our mountain environments continue to deliver social and economic benefits in a changing climate . <p> For almost four decades , New Zealand 's ski industry has seen little major development , even as the climate and mountain environment in which it operates have continued to change . Every successful phase of our skiing history has been driven by innovation , investment and adaptation . History suggests it is time for the next major phase in the evolution of New Zealand skiing . <p> The question is not whether the climate is changing -- it is . The real question is whether we are prepared to respond . We can choose to invest strategically in the next phase of New Zealand 's ski industry through modern infrastructure , improved snowmaking and the careful development of more snow-secure ski terrain , or we can allow our ski @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the Rock and Pillar Range . <p> The snow will continue to fall . Whether future generations continue to enjoy skiing in Aotearoa New Zealand will depend on the choices we make today . <p> Prof Nicolas Cullen is a climate scientist in the School of Geography Te Iho Whenua , University of Otago Otakou Whakaihu Waka and member of He Kaupapa Hononga : Otago 's Climate Change Research Network .

Photo : Reuters <p> A fast-moving wildfire has forced more than 10,000 people from homes and campsites near France 's Atlantic coast , as extreme heat and deepening drought fuelled fires across Europe and scientists warned that climate change was worsening the continent 's water shortages . <p> Across Europe , three heatwaves in succession this season have intensified drought , strained water supplies and left vegetation tinder-dry , helping wildfires scorch more land already this year than the annual average of the past two decades on the world 's fastest-warming continent . @@139251563 <h> The EU re-thinks the value of livestock farming with a much broader perspective , more attuned to the New Zealand approach <p> The European Commission used this image to promote their livestock strategy ! <p> One of the advantages of working in agricultural media is the opportunity to look beyond New Zealand 's borders . While our farming systems are unique , the challenges confronting agriculture are increasingly global . Whether it is climate policy , market volatility , animal health , labour shortages or rising production costs , farmers around the world are searching for the same outcome : profitable businesses that can withstand an uncertain future . <p> That is why the European Union 's latest discussion around livestock farming is worth paying attention to . <p> Despite vastly different production systems and policy settings , many of the conclusions emerging from Europe have direct relevance for New Zealand 's primary sector . <p> The discussion centers on the European Commission 's new livestock strategy , but what makes it interesting is the way the conversation has shifted . Rather than asking whether livestock farming has a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ how livestock systems can become more resilient , more efficient and less dependent on external inputs . That represents an important distinction . <p> For too long , livestock has often been discussed almost exclusively through the lens of emissions and environmental impact . Europe is beginning to recognise that livestock production can not be viewed in isolation from food security , rural economies , biodiversity and strategic resilience . <p> European Commissioner for Agriculture and Food Christoph Hansen was quick to put the industry 's contribution into perspective . Livestock farming , he noted , generates around ? 400 billion in annual turnover and provides employment for approximately seven million people across Europe . At the same time , he acknowledged the sector is under pressure from climate change , animal diseases and increasingly ambitious environmental objectives , contributing to a livestock population that has declined by around 10% over the past decade . <p> Those figures are significant , but the underlying message is even more important . Europe has recognised that reducing livestock numbers without considering the wider consequences creates a different set of challenges . Food @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ magically replace itself . Processing plants , contractors , machinery dealers , transport companies and countless service industries all rely on productive farming businesses . It is a lesson New Zealand should keep firmly in mind as it continues to debate land use , emissions policy and the future of pastoral farming . <p> One of the most interesting conversations in the programme came from a mixed dairy farm in Belgium that fundamentally changed its business following the 2009 milk crisis . Rather than chasing greater scale , the family focused on becoming more self-reliant . Feed is grown on the farm , manure fertilises the soils , livestock harvest their own pasture and direct marketing reduces dependence on external processors . It is a farming system designed to reduce exposure to volatile input costs while improving business resilience . <p> Camille Cosmon captured the philosophy in one simple observation . " It simply does n't make sense for our farming system to depend on soybeans grown on the other side of the world . In many ways , it 's about getting back to basics . What does a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ almost have come from a New Zealand farmer . Our competitive advantage has always been built around efficient pasture utilisation . Yet like much of the developed world , we have gradually become more dependent on imported feed ingredients , fertiliser , fuel and other inputs . There is nothing wrong with adopting new technology or improving productivity , but Europe 's renewed focus on self-sufficiency serves as a timely reminder that resilience often comes from reducing unnecessary dependence rather than continually adding complexity . <p> Technology also features prominently throughout the European discussion , although the emphasis is practical rather than aspirational . France 's largest pig cooperative , Copperl , invests between ? 15 million and ? 20 million annually into research and development , including precision feeding systems that calculate the optimal ration for every individual animal . Feed represents around 70% of production costs , so improving feed efficiency delivers immediate economic benefits while also reducing nutrient losses and lowering reliance on imported protein crops . <p> New Zealand farmers are already travelling a similar path . Precision agriculture , variable-rate application , pasture measurement , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ farm management software are all focused on producing more from fewer resources . The technology itself is not the objective . Better profitability , stronger resilience and improved environmental performance are . That distinction is important because it shifts innovation away from being a compliance exercise and back towards being a business tool . <p> Perhaps the strongest message to emerge from the discussion was the recognition that livestock farming supports far more than the people who own the land . Bertrand Convert from Copperl described the effect livestock has on rural communities in simple terms . A handful of livestock farms in a rural village also supports banks , restaurants , building contractors , electricians , mechanics and countless other businesses . In his view , every livestock farm creates around 10 additional jobs beyond those directly employed on the farm itself . <p> Another aspect of the European strategy that deserves attention is the acknowledgement that there is no single model for successful farming . Hansen argued that Europe 's diversity is one of its strengths and that different farming systems each have a role to play . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ conventional commercial farming all contribute to the overall resilience of the agricultural sector . <p> That is equally true in New Zealand . Intensive dairy farms , hill country sheep and beef operations , mixed livestock enterprises and specialist finishing units all contribute differently to export earnings , environmental outcomes and regional economies . The strength of New Zealand agriculture has never been uniformity . It has been the ability of farmers to adapt their systems to local conditions while continually improving efficiency and productivity . <p> Perhaps the most encouraging aspect of the entire discussion was its tone . <p> Rather than treating livestock as a liability , Europe increasingly appears to be viewing the sector as an asset that must evolve rather than disappear . Hansen himself acknowledged that policymakers have made life unnecessarily difficult for livestock farmers in recent years before concluding with a statement that should resonate on this side of the world as well . " Farmers are entrepreneurs who deserve to make a decent living from what they produce . " <p> That may ultimately be the biggest lesson New Zealand can take from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is unlikely to be secured through ideology , nor through endless debate about choosing between production and sustainability . It will come from recognising that productive farming , environmental stewardship , technological innovation and strong rural communities are not competing objectives . They are all part of the same equation . <p> The countries that succeed over the coming decades will be those that understand livestock is not simply an agricultural industry . It is a strategic national asset that supports food security , regional economies and the prosperity of the communities built around it . <p> Angus Kebbell is a producer at The Weekly Hotwire . @@139303227 <h> If another civilisation lived on Earth before us , would we know ? <p> Caption:Dinosaurs roamed Earth for tens of million of years yet only a few thousand near-complete fossils have been found.Photo credit:AFP / TIMOTHY A. CLARY <p> When astrobiologist Adam Frank walked into the office of NASA scientist Gavin Schmidt , he had what he believed was the weirdest question : do aliens experience climate change ? <p> It took just moments for Schmidt to out-weird him . <p> Frank , a University of Rochester professor who looks for signs of life in the universe , was digging into whether advanced civilisations would inevitably trigger climate shifts , just as humans are doing now . <p> He was looking at other planets , he told Schmidt that day back in 2017 , " because , of course , we know that there have been no other civilisations on Earth . " <p> Schmidt immediately cut him off . " How do you know that ? " he asked . <p> It was a record scratch moment , Frank said , and it started the two scientists off @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on Earth millions of years ago , powered themselves with energy and wiped themselves out , would we know ? Would they have left traces of their presence etched into the record of our planet ? <p> They called the idea the " Silurian hypothesis " - after a fictional advanced race of reptiles in the TV show " Doctor Who " - and published a paper on it in 2018 . <p> It is a mind-bending idea that not only looks deep into the Earth 's past but also far into its future , as humans change the climate swiftly and dramatically by burning fossil fuels . <p> " It is a thought experiment , but one that exposes our potential vulnerability globally , " said Katharine Hayhoe , an atmospheric scientist at Texas Tech University , who was not involved in the research . <p> The Silurean hypothesis opens big questions about the trajectory of advanced civilisations and whether all those that harness energy on a global scale are destined to burn out , or whether there 's a chance to live sustainably and survive for millions of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ exactly the moment that we 're in , " said Frank . " We are pushing the planet into a new climate regime ... we know this is true , right ? And yet we 're not doing anything about it . " <p> The reason Schmidt had been mulling the possibility of previous advanced civilisations on Earth was an event deep in the past . <p> Around 55 million years ago , the planet 's climate signals went haywire . Global temperatures spiked up to 15 degrees Fahrenheit and ecosystems collapsed , as huge amounts of planet-heating carbon were injected into the atmosphere . <p> This period , known as the Paleocene-Eocene Thermal Maximum and first noted in the 1990s , may have been the result of an enormous volcanic eruption , or maybe a sudden release of methane . But exactly why it happened is still something of a mystery . <p> What intrigued Schmidt were the similarities to what 's happening now as humans push up temperatures and drive extinctions . <p> And it 's not the only unexplained change deep in the planet 's past that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of oxygen and sharp hikes in temperature . <p> The likelihood is these are natural climate events , but what if they were n't ? How would we know ? In their report , Frank and Schmidt go through the chain of possible evidence that might exist in support of a past advanced civilisation . <p> Today , only around one percent of the Earth 's surface is urbanised - and it would n't take long for what we 've built to be erased . The further back in time a hypothetical civilisation existed , the harder it would be to find direct evidence , like objects . <p> Over millions of years , the planet is resculpted as tectonic plates shift , volcanoes erupt and land erodes . The Earth 's oldest large-scale stretch of land is the Negev Desert in southern Israel , at around 1.8 million years old . <p> Surface features may not provide much evidence , so the scientists looked at fossils . A vanishingly small fraction of animal species is fossilised , however , and even fewer are ever found . Dinosaurs roamed the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ few thousand near-complete fossils have been discovered . <p> It might be easy to miss a civilisation that only existed for 100,000 years - a mere blip in the 400-million-year-old history of complex life on the planet . <p> The researchers then considered traces left by chemical changes . Think of the Grand Canyon , Frank said , where you can see line after line representing different ages of the Earth . <p> " Every one of those strata is ground up rock that keeps a memory of what the chemistry of the Earth was like then . " If a previous civilisation had altered the planet 's chemistry - as humans have done by releasing vast amounts of carbon pollution - there might be a record . <p> The scientists also considered what other evidence humans might leave behind after our civilisation has ended , like the vast amounts of plastics as well as artificial hormones for agriculture and persistent chemicals , which do not break down in the environment . These might all show up in future sediments , many millions of years from now . As might @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the paper was n't to establish whether the Silurian hypothesis is correct , Frank said . Instead , it was " to say here 's the things you 'd want to look for in a much more high-resolution way in order to see whether or not there was ever a civilisation before us . " <p> Both Frank and Schmidt are in the strange position of having written a study about a theory neither believes in - they just say we can not yet rule it out . <p> No one appears to have picked up the call for a deeper investigation in the years since , but some scientists have raised questions . <p> Jan Zalasiewicz , a geologist , paleontologist and emeritus professor at the University of Leicester in the UK , said the Silurian hypothesis is " wonderful and deeply thought-provoking idea . " <p> But Schmidt and Frank underestimated just how much modern humans have transformed the planet , as well as " the effectiveness of the fossilisation process and how good it is at preserving a history of what has happened on our planet @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ out intelligent pre-human societies , " dinosaurs that could sing complex arias , say , or Jurassic ammonites that could compose poetry , " said Zalasiewicz , who co-wrote the 2025 book " Discarded : How Technofossils Will be Our Ultimate Legacy " . <p> But as soon as cities are built , metals are smelted , plastics are made and discarded - these will leave a clear footprint , he said . <p> Zalasiewicz is comfortable ruling out the prior existence of the kind of advanced civilisation we live in today : " energy-guzzling , manipulative , constructional , and producing waste in colossal quantities . " <p> And that 's the beauty of science , Frank said . " You publish a paper , it 's like , ' Woo , this is great , ' and then somebody comes along and says you 're wrong . " <p> Someone else may find something that points in a different direction , and eventually , as research accumulates , a consensus will be formed . <p> For now , perhaps the most important reason to explore this hypothesis is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ technological civilisations like ours . <p> We 're smart enough to develop technology , but we might not be smart enough to figure out how to deal with its climate-shifting consequences , Frank said . <p> " Are we an experiment that the planet has run , which is going to fail ? "

@@139327373 <p> Aotearoa has an opportunity to step back from the immediate political debate and consider what balance it wants to strike between seafood production and the long-term health of its oceans , writes marine science professor Simon Thrush . <p> The government 's controversial Fisheries Amendment Bill has reignited debate over New Zealand 's most common -- and most controversial -- commercial fishing method . <p> Bottom trawling involves commercial fishing boats dragging large , heavy nets across the seafloor . It is the most widespread source of human disturbance to the global seabed . Around New Zealand , marine scientists have documented its ecological impacts for decades . <p> Although the bill covers a broad range of fisheries management changes , debate has centred on whether it would weaken environmental safeguards that influence how activities such as bottom trawling are managed . <p> After an earlier U-turn on one significant proposal , fisheries minister Shane Jones last month delayed the legislation until after the general election . But many of its more contentious reforms remain on the table and could return under the next parliament . <p> Prime minister @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ need to be balanced against its economic benefits . <p> The commercial fishing industry employs thousands of people and generates around NZ$2 billion in export earnings each year . It is an important export industry , even if smaller than sectors such as international education and comparable in size to wine exports . <p> Still , our research in the marine environment suggests any balance should place greater weight on protecting marine biodiversity and the ecosystem processes that support ocean health . <p> These slow-growing , oceanic forests support a rich diversity of marine life but can take many decades to recover from repeated trawling . <p> The impact on seamounts has received international attention because of the slow recovery potential . But trawling impacts occur across the seafloor . <p> As nets pass across the it , they stir up sediment and cloud the surrounding water . This can smother filter-feeders such as shellfish , sponges and corals while making it harder for other species to feed and survive . <p> Between 1992 and 1993 and 2017 and 2018 , government observers recorded trawl nets catching more than 4,200 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ environmental impacts also extend beyond the species caught in nets . Bottom trawling also disrupts some of the ocean 's most important natural processes . <p> The seafloor is the planet 's largest carbon store , with millions of tonnes locked away beneath New Zealand waters . When trawlers disturb the seabed , some stored carbon may be released into the water column . <p> Researchers are still working to determine how much ultimately contributes to ocean acidification and climate change , but the role of seabed carbon is receiving increasing scientific attention . <p> The seafloor also underpins the health of marine ecosystems . Organic matter settling on the seabed provides food for countless organisms living on and within the sediment . <p> Burrowing creatures recycle nutrients and regulate carbon and nitrogen , supporting the productivity of surrounding waters . When trawlers scrape across the seabed , they disturb these communities and the ecosystem processes they sustain . <p> Industry group Seafood New Zealand also notes that about one third of New Zealand 's exclusive economic zone is closed to bottom trawling , while fishers typically operate over only @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ so , the cumulative footprint is substantial . Between 1990 and 2025 , bottom trawling was carried out across nearly half a million square kilometres of New Zealand waters -- an area almost twice the combined size of the North and South islands . <p> That suggests there is room to better balance food production with environmental protection . <p> One proposal advanced by marine scientists would protect 50% of New Zealand 's territorial seas and exclusive economic zone from bottom trawling . At present , only about 15% of waters at fishable depths ( shallower than 1,600 metres ) are closed to bottom trawling . <p> Other changes could also help . Environmental assessments of previously unfished areas before trawling begins , establishing trawl corridors that avoid the most ecologically important habitats , and creating wider buffer zones around marine protected areas could all reduce impacts while allowing commercial fishing to continue . <p> With the Fisheries Amendment Bill now on hold , New Zealand has an opportunity to step back from the immediate political debate and consider what balance it wants to strike between seafood production and the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ simply an environmental objective , protecting marine ecosystems is also an investment in the long-term resilience of the fisheries that depend on them . A fresh approach could give both marine ecosystems and the fishing industry a better chance to thrive into the future . <h> Members <h> Subscribe <h> FOLLOW US <h> DOWNLOAD THE APP <p> The Spinoff is subject to NZ Media Council procedures . @@139327555 <h> Former trade minister Tim Groser discusses latest US tariffs <p> On 9 November 1989 , after months of escalating protests , an East German official mistakenly announced on live television that citizens could travel unimpeded to the West , immediately . <p> Within hours , hordes of citizens had stormed the border checkpoints , swamping the guards who had little option but to simply open the gates . <p> After decades of food shortages , long lines for consumer goods and failing infrastructure , it was bookmarked as the end of the Cold War and the penultimate victory of capitalism over centrally planned economies . <p> Communism was pronounced dead . <p> Within two years , the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics was dissolved , Mikhail Gorbachev had stood aside and , with China a largely agrarian nation in the early throes of its economic transformation , Western leaders had every reason to feel triumphant . <p> Not so now . <p> In just 35 years , Beijing 's leaders have plotted a course that has seen China emerge as the undisputed leader of industrial production with almost complete @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ planet and in manufacturing . <p> In a world trying to grapple with the best way to wean itself off fossil fuels , China 's automotive industry is poised to blow away global competition on electric vehicles following the West 's ill-conceived reliance on internal combustion engines . <p> Not surprisingly , it is also the dominant player in electrification . Photo credit:AFP / ALAIN JOCARD <h> Live update on wildfires in France and Spain <p> Firefighters battling devastating fires in France have had to contend with a new threat : immense fire clouds that create their own wind and even lightning that can spark fresh blazes . <p> Known as " fire clouds " or pyrocumulonimbus clouds , these ominous-looking thunderstorms are created by extreme wildfires and can exacerbate already extremely dangerous conditions . <p> This phenomenon , which is new to France , is yet another consequence of human-caused climate change , according to French researcher Jean-Baptiste Filippi , who studies wildfires . <p> As firefighters in southwest France struggle to bring a forest inferno under control before yet another @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the phenomenon . <h> What is a fire cloud ? <p> Filippi : It is a storm where a column of air forms above a fire . <p> It starts off as a cute little white cloud - a cumulus cloud , which is formed because water vapour has turned into tiny droplets . <p> As the sun heats the ground , the water vapour suddenly condenses . The cumulus becomes a much larger cloud , containing thousands of tonnes of suspended water . <p> Firefighters operate next to flames at a residential area during a wildfire near Lege Cap Ferret , southwestern France on July 23 , 2026 . <p> AFP / JULIEN DE ROSA <p> Then , when the surrounding air has become heated enough , it forms a column with an anvil-shaped cloud - the cumulonimbus . <p> This condenses even more air and generates more energy , with updrafts of more than 150 kilometres an hour . <p> It then rains , but the rain evaporates before it reaches the ground . <p> But it still cools the atmosphere where it falls , creating strong downdrafts @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a common phenomenon in Portugal , California and Australia , where firefighters are more used to them . In Portugal , they had been rare until the deadly 2017 fires in Pedrogao Grande changed things . <p> In France , pyrocumulonimbus clouds are quite new . There were some in Landiras south of Bordeaux in 2022 , but not for long . <p> A firefighter sprays water on a burning forest close to a residential area during a wildfire near Lege-Cap-Ferret , southwestern France , on July 23 , 2026 . <p> AFP / JULIEN DE ROSA <p> Therefore , we need to learn how to manage fires that creates their own wind - this is something relatively new . <h> Is climate change to blame ? <p> Yes , it is . A consequence of global warming is more frequent heatwaves . While thunderstorms are common in the region of this fire , thunderstorm conditions after three successive heatwaves is a new combination . <h> Are they predictable ? <p> Weather services can predict thunderstorms . But because these storms are located directly above a fire , we would @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . That 's impossible . <p> We can predict the conditions that will lead to these fires , but we wo n't know exactly where they are . <p> The biggest challenge is how unpredictable they are . The fire in France 's southwest Gironde region started with northerly winds , then westerly , then southerly , then easterly , then back again to the south , then the west ... <p> On top of that , there is this potential energy in the atmosphere which meant that , locally , the convection column sucked up the embers and carried the fire further afield . <p> The local conditions became catastrophic and the rate of the fire 's spread was staggering .

@@139375298 <p> The Government 's proposal to prevent climate-related lawsuits against major greenhouse gas emitters could have consequences extending well beyond New Zealand 's courts , with one of the country 's leading legal scholars warning it may also undermine the nation 's international climate commitments and obligations under its free trade agreement with the EU . Emeritus Professor Jane Kelsey of the ... <p> The Government 's proposal to prevent climate-related lawsuits against major greenhouse gas emitters could have consequences extending well beyond New Zealand 's courts , with one of the country 's leading legal scholars warning it may also undermine the nation 's international climate commitments and obligations under its free trade agreement with the EU . <p> Emeritus Professor Jane Kelsey of the University of Auckland Law School says legislation currently under consideration raises important questions about whether New Zealand is meeting the environmental standards it has committed to under international agreements . <p> The proposed law would prevent climate-related tort claims from being brought against companies over their greenhouse gas emissions . It follows the landmark legal action initiated by Northland kaumatua Mike Smith against several @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ New Zealand 's most closely watched climate proceedings . <p> The Government argues the legislation is intended to provide certainty for businesses by ensuring climate policy is determined through Parliament rather than the courts . Supporters say emissions reductions should continue to be managed through existing regulatory frameworks , including the Emissions Trading Scheme and climate legislation , rather than through civil litigation . <p> Professor Kelsey , however , says the proposal has implications that reach beyond domestic policy . She argues the European Union -- New Zealand Free Trade Agreement includes commitments requiring both parties to effectively implement climate obligations and uphold environmental protections . <p> The agreement contains chapters covering sustainable development , climate change and environmental cooperation . While they are designed to encourage collaboration rather than impose automatic trade sanctions , Professor Kelsey says weakening legal accountability for greenhouse gas emissions could invite increased scrutiny from New Zealand 's international trading partners . <p> The European Union has increasingly linked trade policy with climate action , embedding environmental standards into its modern trade agreements and introducing measures such as the Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ continued confidence in the country 's environmental credentials is considered increasingly important as overseas markets place greater emphasis on sustainability , emissions reduction and environmental governance . <p> The proposed legislation has also generated debate over access to justice . Critics argue climate litigation provides communities with an avenue to seek accountability where they believe significant environmental harm has occurred , while supporters of the bill maintain climate policy should remain the responsibility of elected governments rather than the judiciary . <p> For Maori , the debate carries additional significance . Climate change has disproportionate impacts on many iwi and hapu through coastal erosion , flooding , changing weather patterns and risks to whenua , wai and mahinga kai . Questions have also been raised about how the legislation aligns with Te Tiriti o Waitangi obligations and Maori interests in environmental stewardship . <p> The bill passed its first reading in Parliament earlier this year and is expected to face further scrutiny as it progresses through the legislative process . As debate continues , legal experts say Parliament will need to balance business certainty , climate accountability , international obligations @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ addressing climate change . <p> Whether the legislation ultimately strengthens investment confidence or creates new legal and diplomatic challenges is likely to remain a significant issue as New Zealand continues implementing both its domestic climate policies and its international trade commitments . 
@@139387192 <p> The reactions to New Zealand 's latest emissions monitoring report were vastly different depending who you heard it from . Prime minister Christopher Luxon was calm and confident , telling journalists New Zealand was on " on track " to deliver its next two emissions budgets , even suggesting that the country may reach net zero emissions " a few years earlier " than the official target date of 2050 . That stood in stark contrast to Green co-leader Chloe Swarbrick , who , upon reading the same report as Luxon , concluded that " the government has absolutely screwed it " . <p> The Climate Change Commission , the independent Crown entity that authored the report , did n't seem to share Luxon 's optimism either . Chief executive Jo Hendy said the report was " a clear warning sign " , and the government 's current plans and policies were " insufficient " to meet future budgets or the 2050 target . <p> " Emissions are gradually falling but progress stalled in 2024 , and current policy settings are not delivering at the pace needed . Government @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ getting the country back on track , " she said . <p> What are emissions budgets ? <p> The emissions budgets are five-year targets which are intended to set the course towards the goal of zero net emissions by 2050 , which New Zealand committed to through the Paris Agreement and Zero Carbon Act . The budgets are set as the maximum net emissions New Zealand can emit over a five-year period . <p> The first emissions budget , covering 2022-2025 , called for an annual average of no more than 72.5 megatonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent ( Mt CO2e ) per year . The next budget period , set for 2026-2030 drops to an average of 61Mt per year , and the 2031-35 budget drops even further to 48Mt per year . <p> What has happened so far ? <p> First , the good news : it looks like New Zealand will meet its first emissions budget . The data for 2025 has n't been fully finalised but the early projections in the report suggest that New Zealand will be under the cap with 7.7Mt to spare . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ CO2e , down from 61.5 Mt in 2019 , a fall of 10.8% . Gross emissions were down 9.4% over the same period . <p> The commission 's report warned that the second emissions budget covering 2026-2030 was " at significant risk , and that risk has increased in the last year " , and that the " current plans and policies are insufficient to meet the third budget or the 2050 target " . <p> It found that " most areas of emissions reductions are now at significant risk " and that New Zealand needed to " more than double " the current pace of decarbonisation to have any chance of reaching net zero by 2050 . <p> Exactly what that would involve differs from sector to sector . <p> Electricity <p> Emissions from electricity have been gradually falling , with an exception in 2024 . The report is positive about changes to the planning system , such as RMA reform , which should enable more construction of solar and wind farms . It warns that New Zealand still lacks low-emission electricity generation options to rely on during dry @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ reduction in electricity generation will require a long-term roadmap of renewable energy investment . <p> Transport <p> Transport emissions , which make up 18.1% of New Zealand 's gross emissions , have declined by 10% since peaking in 2018 . However , the Climate Change Commission warned that some recent government policy decisions could lead to higher emissions , most notably the changes to and potential removal of the Clean Vehicle Standard and delays in installing EV charging ports . <p> Industry <p> Total industrial energy use has fallen by 27.3% since 2019 , though this is largely due to lower production rather than widespread adoption of clean technology . But the report was more positive about this sector than any other . It suggested that industrial emissions would continue to fall due to support from the Government Investment in Decarbonising Industry Fund . Reaching the 2050 target would " depend heavily on policy credibility " , particularly the Emissions Trading Scheme , to incentivise action from large businesses . <p> Agriculture <p> Emissions from agriculture , primarily methane from livestock and manure , are not included in the net @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a 14% to 24% reduction in biogenic methane by 2050 . This was lowered in 2025 from an initial target of a 24% to 47% reduction . <p> The report found that the 2030 biogenic methane target was no longer projected to be met . Even with these less ambitious settings , current policies were " insufficient " and there was a " significant risk " of missing other future targets . <p> The farming industry has long held up technological improvements such as new feed types as a panacea , but the commission warned that these solutions had a high degree of uncertainty . It suggested this risk could be managed by " strengthening the transition to high-value , low-emissions land uses and other mitigation options " . <p> Forestry <p> The amount of carbon emissions removed by forests has steadily increased after a concerted increase in planting . However , the report warned that " disproportionate reliance on forests " was a risk in itself . Additional planting would not help New Zealand reach its 2030 targets due to the low level of carbon absorption from young trees @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , and its authors are appropriately urgent with their recommendations . It calls for the government to take action within the next year to strengthen the Emissions Trading Scheme , scale up support measures for agriculture , and incentivise more electric vehicle and public transport use . <p> As for Luxon 's claims that New Zealand is " on track " to meet its targets , the report recommended that the government update its approach to emissions projections because " the assumptions underlying the projections do not appear to be driven by a realistic assessment of current policy " . Climate change minister Simon Watts said the government would formally respond to the report in October . <h> Members <h> Subscribe <h> FOLLOW US <h> DOWNLOAD THE APP <p> The Spinoff is subject to NZ Media Council procedures . @@139387354 <h> Hide <h> Blazes burning with the intensity of ' atomic bombs ' : Europe enters a new era of mega fires <p> Laura Paddison for <p> Caption:Smoke and flames from a wildfire emanate from burning trees and vegetation of a forest near Cotignac , southeastern France on 23 July 23 . @@139387619 <p> UK politician , Alistair Carmichael says that there was the positive spirit and mood in NZ farming . <p> A leading British farming politician says he 's impressed with the positive attitude of New Zealand farmers that he met during his recent trip here . <p> Alistair Carmichael , who heads Britain 's influential Environment , Food and Rural Affairs Select Committee in the British Parliament , and some of his colleagues , were on a brief study tour of NZ to get a firsthand look at our farming system . Carmichael is a lawyer and owns a family cattle and sheep farm on the island of Islay in the Scottish Hebrides . <p> During his trip he met senior NZ politicians , farming leaders , visited farms in the Waikato and attended the National Fieldays . <p> " What I saw there was the positive spirit and mood in NZ farming , which is in sharp contrast to what the situation is like back home . There , people are feeling a bit under the cosh , milk prices are low , meat prices have been high and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ get off the farm for slaughter and poultry is being pushed from pillar to post trying to make a profit , " he says . <p> Carmichael says the most valuable conversations he had in NZ were with farmers , such as in the beer tent at Fieldays and on the farms he visited . He says these people have real skin in the game , in contrast to many of the policy makers and consultants where often the messages are ' filtered ' . <p> He says the big issue now for the UK is food security and one of his reasons for coming to NZ was to look at the structural changes it made during the 1980s with the removal of agricultural subsidies . He notes that England has almost no production subsidies and the only ones it has are for environmental improvement and nature restoration . <p> " Conversely , while Scotland , Wales and Northern Ireland are on the same journey , they still have subsidies that are more production focused , " he says . <p> Although Carmichael admires the move by NZ to remove @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ this model could be imposed on or work in the UK . He says the system worked for NZ in the 1980s , but 45 years on , the world is in a different space . He says while the move to bigger farms created efficiencies in NZ , in the UK and in other parts of Europe there is a mandate to protect smaller communities and preserve the natural environment . <p> " Preserving the smaller farming units , mostly run by single families , is an important part of the social structure of rural communities . Focusing on financial imperatives can often bring other unintended consequences on the way that people have to live . In the UK we can make political decisions about how we reform what is important to us . For example , how do we keep people farming in upland areas ? It 's about trade-offs about what things do we preserve as well as the things that we want to change , " he says . <h> The Policy Maker Problem <p> Carmichael says a major problem for farmers in Britain are policy @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ policies they make . He says this particularly applies to policy around climate change . <p> " For example , it was suggested in Scotland last year that we should reduce our livestock numbers by 35% . That would be the end of red meat production in Scotland and the end of communities in rural Scotland . They have also suggested that we could meet our food needs by imports - this is lunacy . <p> " So , this could be done it seems by importing all our food from , say Brazil , where beef has been produced on land that was reclaimed rain forest and probably to lower animal welfare standards , " he says . <p> Carmichael says they seem to be missing the point that the climate crisis is a global issue and what they are suggesting is madness . <p> While most of Carmichael 's time is devoted to politics as the MP for Orkney and Shetland , he keeps a close eye on the family farm where his elderly father still lives . He says it 's important that farming is done in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ his own farm . He says at one stage the farm was home to a rare breed of birds called Choughs that nest on the farm or nearby cliffs . He says these birds like to fossick around cow pats . <p> " My father got rid of the cattle and immediately the Choughs disappeared . So , I made a conscious decision to bring back cattle , which I like anyway , and suddenly the Choughs returned . This is all about respecting nature in the process of commercially farming a property , " he says . <p> For Carmichael , the take-home message from NZ is that what it has done is good for NZ , but it 's critical to have sensible workable policies that take into account the special needs of rural communities . This can only be achieved , he says , by getting direct input from farmers and having policy makers who have hands on experience of farming and who have not spent a lifetime confined to an office in the city . <p> OPINION : At Fieldays this year , one theme kept @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ something that happens " over there " . They are now translating quickly into local , farm-level impacts through increasingly complex and connected channels . <p> Signing up to a benchmarking tool is not about competition , but about improving collectively , says Ximena Puig and Alvaro Luzardo who are 50-50 sharemilkers at a 164-hectare ( effective ) dairy farm in Eketahuna in the Lower North Island .
